      "description": "Comprehensive mathematical learning document on estimating the probability distortion field",
      "file_text": "# Estimating the Probability Distortion\n\nA learning document on the mathematics of measuring how training reshapes a language model's output distribution.\n\n---\n\n## Part 1: What we are trying to measure\n\nA language model defines a conditional probability distribution. Given an input prompt $x$ (a sequence of tokens), it assigns probabilities to possible output sequences $y$. We write this as $P(y \\mid x; \\theta)$, where $\\theta$ are the model's parameters.\n\nTwo distributions are of central interest:\n\n**The base distribution.** Call it $P_{\\text{base}}(y \\mid x)$. This is what the model produces after pretraining only \u2014 exposure to a large corpus of text without any subsequent alignment training. The parameters at this point are $\\theta_0$.\n\n**The post-training distribution.** Call it $P_{\\text{inst}}(y \\mid x)$. This is what the model produces after Stage 2 training: supervised fine-tuning, reinforcement learning from human feedback, constitutional AI, or whatever other alignment procedures are applied. The parameters at this point are $\\theta_T$, the training endpoint.\n\nThe object we care about is the *probability distortion field*:\n\n$$\\rho(x, y) = \\log P_{\\text{inst}}(y \\mid x) - \\log P_{\\text{base}}(y \\mid x).$$\n\nThis is a function over the entire space of (prompt, completion) pairs. Its value at any specific $(x, y)$ tells us how much more (or less) likely that completion has become as a result of training. Where $\\rho > 0$, training has amplified the probability of completion $y$ given prompt $x$. Where $\\rho < 0$, training has suppressed it. Where $\\rho = 0$, training has not changed it.\n\nThe whole framework is about understanding the structure of $\\rho$. Everything that follows develops the mathematical apparatus needed to predict, measure, and interpret this single object.\n\n### Why $\\rho$ is the right thing\n\nA few quick justifications, since this is not the obvious choice.\n\nWe could compare $P_{\\text{inst}}$ and $P_{\\text{base}}$ directly \u2014 looking at the difference $P_{\\text{inst}}(y \\mid x) - P_{\\text{base}}(y \\mid x)$. This is mathematically allowed but practically bad. Probabilities of specific long sequences are tiny; differences between them are tinier still and dominated by sampling noise. The log-ratio is much better-behaved because it's invariant to overall scale and combines additively across independent components.\n\nWe could compare in some kind of summary form \u2014 entropy, mean reward, downstream task performance. These miss the per-completion structure that the framework needs. Two distributions can have the same entropy while differing wildly in *which* completions get probability mass. We want to see exactly which completions have been moved.\n\nWe could compare embedding-level representations rather than output probabilities. This gets at internal mechanisms but loses the connection to what the model actually says. The framework is about the output behavior \u2014 what gets produced when users interact with the model \u2014 and $\\rho$ is the natural object at that level.\n\nMost importantly, $\\rho$ has a deep mathematical structure that emerges from how training works. We will see in Section 2 that for the standard form of alignment training (reverse-KL-regularized RLHF), $\\rho$ has an explicit closed form in terms of the reward function. This isn't an analogy or a heuristic; it's an exact result. The choice of $\\rho$ as central object lets us bring serious mathematical machinery to bear on it.\n\n### Three regimes\n\nBefore going into the math, here is what we will find about the structure of $\\rho$.\n\n**Refusal regime.** For some prompts, $\\rho$ has a single sharp positive spike on a specific template completion (\"I cannot help with that,\" or similar). The spike is large enough that the template effectively has all the probability mass after training. $P_{\\text{base}}$ on the same prompt was diffuse across many possible answers; $P_{\\text{inst}}$ is concentrated on the refusal. This is a discrete behavior \u2014 the prompt either triggers the refusal gate or it doesn't.\n\n**Induced-distortion regime.** For other prompts, $\\rho$ has a paired structure: large negative values on truthful completions that the base distribution gave substantial probability to, and large positive values on alternative framings that the base distribution had at low probability. After training, the truthful completions have been pushed toward zero probability and the alternative framings have been amplified to be the modal output. This is the regime where what the model says systematically differs from what the base substrate would have said, in directions that reflect the training signal.\n\n**Untouched regime.** For most prompts, $\\rho \\approx 0$ across all completions with substantial base probability. Training did not engage on these prompts. $P_{\\text{inst}} \\approx P_{\\text{base}}$ up to small fluctuations.\n\nThe mathematical apparatus we develop is needed to make these statements precise and quantitative \u2014 to predict where each regime applies, how strongly $\\rho$ acts, and what observable signatures distinguish them.\n\n---\n\n## Part 2: The exponential tilt \u2014 closed form for KL-regularized RLHF\n\nThe simplest case where we can write $\\rho$ explicitly is reverse-KL-regularized reinforcement learning from human feedback. This is the standard formulation used in production alignment training (it's what Christiano et al. 2017 introduced and what InstructGPT, ChatGPT, Claude, and similar systems are built on). We work it out from scratch.\n\n### The optimization problem\n\nAlignment training has a reward model $r(x, y)$ that scores each (prompt, completion) pair. Higher reward is better. The training procedure adjusts the model to produce higher-rewarded completions, subject to a constraint that the resulting policy doesn't drift too far from the reference (usually post-SFT) policy. In the language of optimization:\n\nLet $\\pi_{\\text{ref}}(y \\mid x)$ be the reference policy (the model before RL fine-tuning). Let $\\pi(y \\mid x)$ be the policy we are optimizing. The objective is:\n\n$$\\max_\\pi \\; \\mathbb{E}_{x \\sim \\mathcal{D}, \\, y \\sim \\pi(\\cdot \\mid x)}[r(x, y)] \\; - \\; \\beta \\, D_{\\text{KL}}\\big(\\pi(\\cdot \\mid x) \\,\\|\\, \\pi_{\\text{ref}}(\\cdot \\mid x)\\big).$$\n\nThe first term rewards generating high-reward completions. The second term penalizes drifting from the reference. The parameter $\\beta > 0$ controls the strength of the regularization. Larger $\\beta$ keeps the policy close to reference; smaller $\\beta$ allows more aggressive optimization for reward.\n\n### Solving by variational calculus\n\nWe want the policy $\\pi^*$ that maximizes this objective. Hold $x$ fixed and treat $\\pi(\\cdot \\mid x)$ as a probability distribution over $y$. We need this distribution to satisfy two constraints:\n\n- Non-negativity: $\\pi(y \\mid x) \\geq 0$ for all $y$.\n- Normalization: $\\sum_y \\pi(y \\mid x) = 1$.\n\nThe objective expanded for fixed $x$ is:\n\n$$L[\\pi] = \\sum_y \\pi(y \\mid x) \\, r(x, y) \\; - \\; \\beta \\sum_y \\pi(y \\mid x) \\log \\frac{\\pi(y \\mid x)}{\\pi_{\\text{ref}}(y \\mid x)}.$$\n\nTo maximize subject to the normalization constraint, we use a Lagrange multiplier $\\lambda$:\n\n$$\\mathcal{L}[\\pi] = L[\\pi] - \\lambda \\left( \\sum_y \\pi(y \\mid x) - 1 \\right).$$\n\nTake the functional derivative with respect to $\\pi(y \\mid x)$ and set it to zero:\n\n$$\\frac{\\partial \\mathcal{L}}{\\partial \\pi(y \\mid x)} = r(x, y) - \\beta \\log \\frac{\\pi(y \\mid x)}{\\pi_{\\text{ref}}(y \\mid x)} - \\beta - \\lambda = 0.$$\n\nRearranging:\n\n$$\\log \\frac{\\pi(y \\mid x)}{\\pi_{\\text{ref}}(y \\mid x)} = \\frac{r(x, y) - \\beta - \\lambda}{\\beta} = \\frac{r(x, y)}{\\beta} - 1 - \\frac{\\lambda}{\\beta}.$$\n\nExponentiating:\n\n$$\\pi(y \\mid x) = \\pi_{\\text{ref}}(y \\mid x) \\cdot \\exp\\left( \\frac{r(x, y)}{\\beta} \\right) \\cdot e^{-1 - \\lambda/\\beta}.$$\n\nThe factor $e^{-1 - \\lambda/\\beta}$ doesn't depend on $y$. Call it $1/Z(x)$, where $Z(x)$ is determined by normalization:\n\n$$Z(x) = \\sum_y \\pi_{\\text{ref}}(y \\mid x) \\exp\\left( \\frac{r(x, y)}{\\beta} \\right).$$\n\nThis is the partition function, by direct analogy with statistical mechanics.\n\nThe optimal policy is:\n\n$$\\boxed{\\pi^*(y \\mid x) = \\frac{1}{Z(x)} \\pi_{\\text{ref}}(y \\mid x) \\exp\\left( \\frac{r(x, y)}{\\beta} \\right).}$$\n\n### Recognizing this\n\nThis is exactly the *exponential tilt* of the reference distribution \u2014 the same structure that appears in statistical mechanics as the Boltzmann distribution. If you set $\\pi_{\\text{ref}}$ to uniform (1/N over N possible states), you recover the Boltzmann distribution $\\pi^*(y) \\propto \\exp(-\\beta E(y))$ with $E = -r$ playing the role of energy and $\\beta$ the inverse temperature. With non-uniform reference, you get the *tilted* distribution that's standard in importance sampling, large deviation theory, and statistical inference.\n\nThe same form appears in:\n\n- Bayesian inference: posterior $\\propto$ prior $\\times$ likelihood.\n- Maximum entropy with constraints: distributions of the form $p \\propto e^{-\\sum_i \\lambda_i f_i}$.\n- Free energy in statistical mechanics: $F = -\\beta^{-1} \\log Z$.\n- Importance sampling: weighting by $\\exp(\\Delta \\log p)$.\n\nThis isn't an analogy. The mathematical structure is genuinely the same, and we can use the full apparatus from these other fields when analyzing $\\pi^*$.\n\n### Reading off $\\rho$\n\nIf $P_{\\text{inst}} = \\pi^*$ and $P_{\\text{base}} = \\pi_{\\text{ref}}$ (we'll come back to whether this identification is exact), then the probability distortion is:\n\n$$\\rho(x, y) = \\log P_{\\text{inst}}(y \\mid x) - \\log P_{\\text{base}}(y \\mid x) = \\frac{r(x, y)}{\\beta} - \\log Z(x).$$\n\nThe reweighting is the reward divided by the regularization strength, minus a normalization constant that depends only on $x$. This is a complete characterization of $\\rho$ in terms of the reward function \u2014 given the reward function $r$, we can write down the distortion $\\rho$ exactly.\n\nThe normalization $\\log Z(x)$ doesn't depend on which completion $y$ we ask about, so for *relative* distortion within a prompt \u2014 comparing how $\\rho$ differs across completions of the same prompt \u2014 we have:\n\n$$\\rho(x, y) - \\rho(x, y') = \\frac{r(x, y) - r(x, y')}{\\beta}.$$\n\nThis is exact. The relative distortion between two completions of the same prompt is exactly the relative reward, scaled by $1/\\beta$.\n\n### What this tells us\n\nThe distortion $\\rho$ is, mathematically, the (rescaled) reward function the institution trained against. Measuring $\\rho^*$ (the empirical distortion field) is reverse-engineering the reward signal that the institution used. Whatever shaping is present in $\\rho$ reflects what the reward model preferred and disfavored.\n\nThis is the connection between empirical measurement and institutional choice. The reward function isn't usually disclosed by institutions, but $\\rho^*$ is recoverable from sampling. So measuring $\\rho^*$ tells us, up to a constant per prompt, what the reward structure was.\n\nA few caveats:\n\nThe identification $P_{\\text{base}} = \\pi_{\\text{ref}}$ is exact only if alignment training is purely the RL stage with KL regularization back to base. In practice, there's often supervised fine-tuning (SFT) before RL, which changes the reference. The full pipeline produces a $\\pi_{\\text{ref}}$ that's already been moved away from $P_{\\text{base}}$. So the $\\rho$ we measure between base and final post-training picks up both effects: SFT changes plus RL-with-KL changes. We can decompose if we have access to intermediate checkpoints (the post-SFT pre-RL model), and not otherwise.\n\nThe closed form assumes we've reached the optimum of the regularized objective. Actual training is iterative and may terminate before convergence. The closed form is the asymptotic target, not necessarily the exact endpoint.\n\nThe closed form assumes the reward function is fixed during training. In practice, reward models are trained on human preference data and have their own errors. The effective $r$ is what the reward model actually outputs, which may differ from the institution's stated objective.\n\nThese complications matter for precision but don't change the central structure. The exponential tilt is the fundamental form. Real training produces approximations to it.\n\n### Multiple stages\n\nIf alignment training has multiple stages \u2014 SFT, then RL, then more SFT, then more RL \u2014 each stage produces its own tilt. The composition is:\n\n$$P_{\\text{inst}} = P_{\\text{base}} \\cdot \\prod_{i} \\exp(\\rho_i)$$\n\nwhere $\\rho_i$ is the distortion introduced by stage $i$, normalized appropriately. The total distortion is the sum:\n\n$$\\rho = \\sum_i \\rho_i.$$\n\nEach stage's distortion is governed by its own reward signal (or labeled examples, in the SFT case). The total distortion accumulates additively in the log domain.\n\nThis decomposition is useful when we have access to intermediate checkpoints. We can compute $\\rho_1$ (post-SFT minus base), $\\rho_2$ (post-RL minus post-SFT), and so on. Each piece reveals what that particular stage contributed.\n\nWhen we don't have checkpoints, we measure only the total $\\rho$ and have to use other diagnostics to attribute pieces to stages.\n\n---\n\n## Part 3: How $\\rho$ arises from training dynamics\n\nThe closed form in Part 2 tells us what $\\rho$ is at the optimum. But it doesn't tell us *how* training gets there, or what happens along the way. Several questions are unaddressed:\n\nWhat is the training trajectory in parameter space?\n\nHow fast does $\\rho$ build up during training, and in what order across topics?\n\nWhen training doesn't reach the optimum, what's the actual $\\rho$ at the realized endpoint?\n\nFor these we need to look at the training dynamics directly.\n\n### Gradient flow\n\nThe simplest model of training is gradient flow. The parameters evolve according to:\n\n$$\\frac{d\\theta_t}{dt} = \\nabla_\\theta \\mathcal{R}(\\theta_t)$$\n\nwhere $\\mathcal{R}(\\theta)$ is the training objective (the KL-regularized reward in our case). This is the continuous-time limit of gradient ascent with infinitesimal step size. For our purposes:\n\n$$\\mathcal{R}(\\theta) = \\mathbb{E}_{x, y \\sim \\pi_\\theta}[r(x, y)] - \\beta \\, \\mathbb{E}_x \\big[ D_{\\text{KL}}(\\pi_\\theta \\| \\pi_{\\text{ref}}) \\big].$$\n\nThe gradient with respect to $\\theta$ is computed via the policy gradient theorem (this is standard but worth tracking through).\n\n### Computing the gradient\n\nWe need $\\nabla_\\theta \\mathbb{E}_{y \\sim \\pi_\\theta}[r(x, y)]$. The expectation depends on $\\theta$ through $\\pi_\\theta$, so we use the log-derivative trick:\n\n$$\\nabla_\\theta \\mathbb{E}_{y \\sim \\pi_\\theta}[r(x, y)] = \\nabla_\\theta \\sum_y \\pi_\\theta(y \\mid x) r(x, y) = \\sum_y r(x, y) \\nabla_\\theta \\pi_\\theta(y \\mid x).$$\n\nThe trick is to write $\\nabla_\\theta \\pi_\\theta = \\pi_\\theta \\cdot \\nabla_\\theta \\log \\pi_\\theta$, so:\n\n$$\\nabla_\\theta \\mathbb{E}_{y \\sim \\pi_\\theta}[r(x, y)] = \\sum_y \\pi_\\theta(y \\mid x) r(x, y) \\nabla_\\theta \\log \\pi_\\theta(y \\mid x) = \\mathbb{E}_{y \\sim \\pi_\\theta}[r(x, y) \\nabla_\\theta \\log \\pi_\\theta(y \\mid x)].$$\n\nFor the KL term:\n\n$$\\nabla_\\theta D_{\\text{KL}}(\\pi_\\theta \\| \\pi_{\\text{ref}}) = \\nabla_\\theta \\mathbb{E}_{y \\sim \\pi_\\theta}[\\log \\pi_\\theta(y) - \\log \\pi_{\\text{ref}}(y)].$$\n\nAfter the same log-derivative manipulation and using $\\nabla_\\theta \\mathbb{E}_{y \\sim \\pi_\\theta}[\\log \\pi_\\theta] = \\mathbb{E}[\\nabla_\\theta \\log \\pi_\\theta]$ (since the score function has mean zero), one gets:\n\n$$\\nabla_\\theta D_{\\text{KL}}(\\pi_\\theta \\| \\pi_{\\text{ref}}) = \\mathbb{E}_{y \\sim \\pi_\\theta}\\big[(\\log \\pi_\\theta(y) - \\log \\pi_{\\text{ref}}(y)) \\nabla_\\theta \\log \\pi_\\theta(y)\\big].$$\n\nSo the gradient of the full objective is:\n\n$$\\nabla_\\theta \\mathcal{R}(\\theta) = \\mathbb{E}_{x, y \\sim \\pi_\\theta}\\Big[\\big(r(x, y) - \\beta \\log \\tfrac{\\pi_\\theta(y \\mid x)}{\\pi_{\\text{ref}}(y \\mid x)}\\big) \\nabla_\\theta \\log \\pi_\\theta(y \\mid x)\\Big].$$\n\nThe thing in parentheses is the *advantage* \u2014 reward minus KL penalty. It's positive on completions the policy should produce more of and negative on completions it should produce less of. The score function $\\nabla_\\theta \\log \\pi_\\theta$ points in parameter directions that increase the probability of the sampled completion. Multiplying them and averaging gives the policy-improvement direction.\n\n### The trajectory\n\nThe gradient flow $d\\theta_t/dt = \\nabla_\\theta \\mathcal{R}(\\theta_t)$ traces out a path in parameter space starting from $\\theta_0 = \\theta_{\\text{base}}$ (or wherever training begins). The endpoint $\\theta_T$ depends on:\n\n- Total training time $T$\n- The reward function $r$ (which directions in parameter space are favored)\n- The KL regularization strength $\\beta$ (how tightly the trajectory is pulled back toward $\\pi_{\\text{ref}}$)\n- The structure of the loss landscape (which directions have low resistance, which have barriers)\n\nIn the limit $T \\to \\infty$ with appropriate convergence, $\\theta_T$ approaches the optimum of $\\mathcal{R}$, and we get the closed-form $\\pi^*$ from Part 2. For finite $T$, we get an approximation.\n\n### What this gives us for $\\rho$\n\nAlong the trajectory, we have a one-parameter family of policies $\\pi_t = \\pi_{\\theta_t}$. We can define a time-resolved distortion:\n\n$$\\rho_t(x, y) = \\log \\pi_t(y \\mid x) - \\log \\pi_0(y \\mid x).$$\n\nThis evolves according to:\n\n$$\\frac{d\\rho_t(x, y)}{dt} = \\frac{d}{dt} \\log \\pi_t(y \\mid x) = \\frac{1}{\\pi_t(y \\mid x)} \\frac{d\\pi_t(y \\mid x)}{dt}.$$\n\nSince $\\pi_t = \\pi_{\\theta_t}$ depends on $t$ through $\\theta_t$:\n\n$$\\frac{d\\pi_t(y \\mid x)}{dt} = \\nabla_\\theta \\pi_\\theta(y \\mid x) \\cdot \\frac{d\\theta_t}{dt} = \\pi_t(y \\mid x) \\nabla_\\theta \\log \\pi_t(y \\mid x) \\cdot \\nabla_\\theta \\mathcal{R}(\\theta_t).$$\n\nSo:\n\n$$\\frac{d\\rho_t(x, y)}{dt} = \\nabla_\\theta \\log \\pi_t(y \\mid x) \\cdot \\nabla_\\theta \\mathcal{R}(\\theta_t).$$\n\nThe rate of distortion change at $(x, y)$ is the inner product of two gradients in parameter space: the score function for $(x, y)$, and the training direction. When these are aligned, distortion grows fast at $(x, y)$. When they're orthogonal, $\\rho$ at $(x, y)$ doesn't change. When they're anti-aligned, distortion decreases (the completion is being pushed down).\n\nThis gives us the central observation: **distortion at $(x, y)$ is determined by the alignment between the gradient of $\\log \\pi$ at $(x, y)$ and the cumulative training direction.**\n\nThe total distortion at the end of training is the integral:\n\n$$\\rho(x, y) = \\rho_T(x, y) - \\rho_0(x, y) = \\int_0^T \\nabla_\\theta \\log \\pi_t(y \\mid x) \\cdot \\nabla_\\theta \\mathcal{R}(\\theta_t) \\, dt.$$\n\nThis is exact. It's a path integral along the training trajectory. We will return to this object \u2014 it's the foundation for the path-integral formulation of $\\rho$.\n\n---\n\n## Part 4: First-order theory \u2014 the neural tangent kernel\n\nThe path integral expression for $\\rho$ is exact but hard to work with. We need approximations to extract predictions. The simplest is to linearize around the starting point $\\theta_0$.\n\n### The setup\n\nDefine $f(\\theta) = \\log \\pi_\\theta(y \\mid x)$ for fixed $(x, y)$. Taylor expand around $\\theta_0$:\n\n$$f(\\theta_T) = f(\\theta_0) + \\nabla_\\theta f(\\theta_0) \\cdot \\Delta\\theta + \\tfrac{1}{2} \\Delta\\theta^T \\nabla^2_\\theta f(\\theta_0) \\Delta\\theta + \\cdots$$\n\nwhere $\\Delta\\theta = \\theta_T - \\theta_0$. The distortion is:\n\n$$\\rho(x, y) = f(\\theta_T) - f(\\theta_0) = g(x, y) \\cdot \\Delta\\theta + \\tfrac{1}{2} \\Delta\\theta^T H(x, y) \\Delta\\theta + \\cdots$$\n\nwith $g(x, y) = \\nabla_\\theta \\log \\pi_\\theta(y \\mid x) |_{\\theta_0}$ (the score function at base) and $H(x, y) = \\nabla^2_\\theta \\log \\pi_\\theta(y \\mid x) |_{\\theta_0}$ (the Hessian at base).\n\nTruncating at first order:\n\n$$\\rho(x, y) \\approx g(x, y) \\cdot \\Delta\\theta.$$\n\n### The structure of $\\Delta\\theta$\n\nWhat is $\\Delta\\theta$? It's the cumulative parameter update from training. From gradient flow, $\\Delta\\theta = \\int_0^T \\nabla_\\theta \\mathcal{R}(\\theta_t) dt$. If we approximate by evaluating gradients at $\\theta_0$ throughout training (the \"lazy\" or NTK approximation), we get:\n\n$$\\Delta\\theta_{\\text{NTK}} = \\int_0^T \\nabla_\\theta \\mathcal{R}(\\theta_0) dt = T \\cdot \\nabla_\\theta \\mathcal{R}(\\theta_0).$$\n\nThat's not quite right because the gradient depends on the current policy, which depends on $\\theta$. A better approximation is to write the training update as a sum over the gradients at the *training examples* the optimizer sees:\n\n$$\\Delta\\theta \\approx \\sum_{i} \\eta_i \\cdot a_i \\cdot \\nabla_\\theta \\log \\pi_{\\theta_0}(y_i \\mid x_i) = \\sum_i \\eta_i a_i g(x_i, y_i)$$\n\nwhere the sum runs over training examples (or RL trajectories), $\\eta_i$ is the effective learning rate, and $a_i$ is the advantage signal for example $i$. The score function is evaluated at $\\theta_0$ \u2014 that's the lazy approximation.\n\n### The kernel\n\nSubstituting:\n\n$$\\rho(x, y) \\approx g(x, y) \\cdot \\sum_i \\eta_i a_i g(x_i, y_i) = \\sum_i \\eta_i a_i \\, K\\big((x, y), (x_i, y_i)\\big)$$\n\nwhere:\n\n$$\\boxed{K\\big((x, y), (x', y')\\big) = g(x, y) \\cdot g(x', y') = \\nabla_\\theta \\log \\pi_{\\theta_0}(y \\mid x) \\cdot \\nabla_\\theta \\log \\pi_{\\theta_0}(y' \\mid x').}$$\n\nThis is the *neural tangent kernel*. It's the inner product of score functions in parameter space.\n\nThe formula tells us that the distortion at any test point $(x, y)$ is a weighted sum of kernel values to the training points, where the weights are the training advantages. When $K$ is large between $(x, y)$ and a training point, the distortion at $(x, y)$ tracks what training did at that point. When $K$ is small, the test point is decoupled from training.\n\n### Properties of the kernel\n\nThe kernel is positive semi-definite \u2014 it's an inner product, after all. As such it defines a Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Space (RKHS), and distortion in the NTK regime is a function in that RKHS.\n\nThe kernel is symmetric: $K((x, y), (x', y')) = K((x', y'), (x, y))$.\n\nFor random initialization in the infinite-width limit, the kernel converges to a deterministic kernel that depends only on the architecture and the input distribution, not on the specific random initialization (Jacot, Gabriel, Hongler 2018). This is the original NTK result. For practical pretrained models the kernel isn't quite this clean \u2014 it depends on what was learned during pretraining \u2014 but it still has structure.\n\nThe kernel has eigendecomposition. Its spectrum tells us about how distortion propagates:\n\n$$K\\big((x, y), (x', y')\\big) = \\sum_k \\lambda_k \\phi_k(x, y) \\phi_k(x', y')$$\n\nwhere $\\lambda_k$ are eigenvalues and $\\phi_k$ are eigenfunctions. The eigenfunctions are the natural \"modes\" of the model's representation space. Training perturbations decompose into these modes, and each mode has its own learning dynamics governed by its eigenvalue.\n\n### Propagation of distortion\n\nThe NTK formula tells us how training propagates from training points to test points. If training is concentrated on a small set of (prompt, completion) pairs, the resulting distortion at any other point is determined by the kernel between that point and the training set.\n\nThis means distortion **spreads**. Training on prompts about Wehrmacht ethnic composition produces distortion not just on those exact prompts but on any prompts whose score-function gradient overlaps with the training prompts' gradients. Semantically and structurally similar prompts tend to have aligned gradients \u2014 they activate similar parameters in similar directions \u2014 so the kernel is large between them.\n\nThe framework's claim that institutional shaping spreads coherently across topic clusters is a kernel propagation claim. The institution explicitly trains on a manageable number of examples; the kernel does the rest.\n\n### When NTK is good and when it isn't\n\nThe lazy/NTK approximation requires several things:\n\nThe parameter updates $\\Delta\\theta$ are small \u2014 small enough that the gradients evaluated at $\\theta_0$ are good approximations to the gradients along the trajectory. This is true near initialization in wide networks but increasingly fails as $\\Delta\\theta$ grows.\n\nThe model is in the \"lazy\" regime where features don't change much during training. This is the regime of overparameterized networks doing kernel regression. Modern alignment training is often *not* in this regime \u2014 feature learning is part of how alignment works.\n\nThe training signal doesn't push parameters into regions of high curvature. Where the loss landscape has high curvature in the gradient direction, the linear approximation breaks down quickly.\n\nFor the framework, the consequence is that the NTK formula gives **qualitative predictions about how distortion spreads** that we expect to hold in broad form, but it doesn't give **quantitative magnitudes** that match observation in heavily-trained regions.\n\nWe need to go beyond NTK for quantitative theory. That's the next several sections.\n\n---\n\n## Part 5: Beyond NTK \u2014 feature learning and dynamical mean field theory\n\nThe NTK approximation freezes the kernel at its initialization value. Real training, especially alignment training that significantly reshapes outputs, allows the kernel to evolve. The internal representations of the model \u2014 its features \u2014 change. We need a formalism that captures this.\n\n### The mean-field parameterization\n\nThe standard NTK setup uses a specific parameterization where, in the infinite-width limit, the kernel is fixed and only the output layer effectively trains. This is not the only choice. The \"mean-field\" or \"$\\mu$P\" parameterization (Yang and Hu 2021) chooses the scaling of weights differently, so that even in the infinite-width limit, all layers continue to learn features during training.\n\nThe mathematical statement: under standard parameterization, the per-neuron contribution to the output is $O(1/\\sqrt{N})$ where $N$ is the layer width. Under mean-field parameterization, the per-neuron contribution is $O(1/N)$. The first scaling means individual neurons matter little and the network is well-described by the kernel between collective features. The second scaling means individual neurons matter and feature learning is preserved at infinite width.\n\nFor a two-layer network $f(x; \\theta) = \\frac{1}{N} \\sum_{j=1}^N a_j \\sigma(w_j \\cdot x)$, the mean-field limit replaces the sum over neurons with an integral over a distribution:\n\n$$f(x; \\mu) = \\int a \\, \\sigma(w \\cdot x) \\, d\\mu(a, w).$$\n\nThe training dynamics for the parameters $\\theta = \\{(a_j, w_j)\\}_{j=1}^N$ become a partial differential equation for the distribution $\\mu$ \u2014 specifically a Wasserstein gradient flow on the space of distributions over neurons.\n\n### Why this matters for $\\rho$\n\nIn the mean-field regime, the kernel changes during training. So the propagation of distortion through the kernel is no longer determined by the initial kernel but by some time-evolved kernel. The first-order NTK formula:\n\n$$\\rho(x, y) \\approx \\sum_i \\eta_i a_i K_0\\big((x, y), (x_i, y_i)\\big)$$\n\nbecomes:\n\n$$\\rho(x, y) \\approx \\int_0^T dt \\, \\mathbb{E}_i\\big[ \\eta_i a_i^{(t)} K_t\\big((x, y), (x_i, y_i)\\big) \\big]$$\n\nwhere $K_t$ is the kernel at training time $t$. The kernel and the policy co-evolve.\n\nThis is harder mathematically but addresses a real phenomenon: alignment training does change the model's internal representations. New features emerge that better encode the institutional commitments; existing features get reorganized. The post-training kernel can be quite different from the pre-training kernel.\n\n### Dynamical mean field theory\n\nFor wide neural networks with feature learning, the right tool is dynamical mean field theory (DMFT). This is the formalism developed by Bordelon and Pehlevan (2022) and others. It tracks not just the parameters but the joint distribution over (features at every time, gradients at every time), self-consistently.\n\nThe basic structure: define order parameters that summarize the network state at any time. For a wide network, these are kernels:\n\n$$Q_t(x, x') = \\frac{1}{N} \\sum_j h_j^{(t)}(x) \\, h_j^{(t)}(x')$$\n\n(feature-feature kernel at time $t$) and\n\n$$R_t(x, x') = \\frac{1}{N} \\sum_j g_j^{(t)}(x) \\, g_j^{(t)}(x')$$\n\n(gradient-gradient kernel at time $t$). These satisfy self-consistent equations that you derive by integrating out the random initialization.\n\nFor a deep network, you get a hierarchy of such kernels, one per layer, coupled through the architecture. The equations are matrix-valued ODEs in time.\n\nFor our purposes, the practical content is:\n\nThe distortion $\\rho$ depends on the time-integrated kernels along the trajectory, not just the initial kernel.\n\nThe trajectory has structure \u2014 early training is approximately NTK (lazy) and progresses into feature-learning regimes as parameters drift.\n\nThe time-evolved kernels have eigenstructures that reveal which features the training is creating versus reweighting.\n\n### Practical takeaway\n\nFor the framework's measurement program, the practical use of DMFT is:\n\nWhen alignment training produces strong distortion (large $\\rho$), the standard NTK kernel is wrong. The effective kernel relevant for predicting distortion is the trajectory-averaged kernel, not the initial one.\n\nThe trajectory-averaged kernel is itself measurable from differential probes \u2014 sample $P_{\\text{base}}$ and $P_{\\text{inst}}$ on probe pairs $(x, y), (x', y')$, compute the empirical correlation in $\\rho^*$, and you're estimating the time-averaged kernel directly.\n\nThis means the framework's measurement methodology can use $\\rho^*$ correlations across probes to estimate the effective propagation kernel, without needing access to the actual training trajectory.\n\nThe full DMFT machinery is overkill for most applications. The lesson is that the kernel evolves and so the propagation structure of $\\rho$ is determined by the evolved kernel, which can differ substantially from $K_0$ in heavily-trained regions.\n\n---\n\n## Part 6: The path integral formulation\n\nThe path integral perspective treats the training trajectory as a path through parameter space and writes the distortion as an integral along this path. This is the most general formulation and works in regimes where Taylor expansion at $\\theta_0$ fails.\n\n### The exact expression\n\nWe had this in Part 3:\n\n$$\\rho(x, y) = \\int_0^T \\nabla_\\theta \\log \\pi_t(y \\mid x) \\cdot \\frac{d\\theta_t}{dt} dt.$$\n\nThis is exact along any specific trajectory. No expansion in $\\Delta\\theta$, no approximation. The price is that we need to know the trajectory, or compute averages over trajectories.\n\n### Stochastic training\n\nReal training isn't deterministic gradient flow. It has noise from minibatch SGD, learning rate schedules, optimizer momentum, and random sampling within RL. We model this by adding noise to the gradient flow:\n\n$$\\frac{d\\theta_t}{dt} = \\nabla_\\theta \\mathcal{R}(\\theta_t) + \\xi_t$$\n\nwhere $\\xi_t$ is a Gaussian noise process with some covariance $\\Sigma$. This is a stochastic differential equation.\n\nThe solution is a probability distribution over trajectories, not a single trajectory. We compute averages of observables (like $\\rho$) over this distribution.\n\n### The Onsager-Machlup functional\n\nFor an SDE $d\\theta_t = b(\\theta_t) dt + \\sigma dW_t$, the probability of a specific trajectory $\\theta(\\cdot)$ is given by the Onsager-Machlup density:\n\n$$P[\\theta(\\cdot)] \\propto \\exp\\left( -\\frac{1}{2} \\int_0^T \\big\\| \\sigma^{-1}(\\dot{\\theta}_t - b(\\theta_t)) \\big\\|^2 dt \\right).$$\n\nFor our case with $b(\\theta) = \\nabla_\\theta \\mathcal{R}$ and $\\sigma = \\sigma_{\\text{noise}} I$:\n\n$$P[\\theta(\\cdot)] \\propto \\exp\\left( -\\frac{1}{2\\sigma_{\\text{noise}}^2} \\int_0^T \\big\\| \\dot{\\theta}_t - \\nabla_\\theta \\mathcal{R}(\\theta_t) \\big\\|^2 dt \\right).$$\n\nThis is the *action functional* for trajectories. Trajectories that follow gradient flow exactly (where $\\dot{\\theta}_t = \\nabla_\\theta \\mathcal{R}$) have zero action and are infinitely more likely than trajectories that deviate. Trajectories that stray have action proportional to the squared deviation, summed along the path.\n\n### The expected distortion\n\nThe expected $\\rho$ over training is:\n\n$$\\mathbb{E}[\\rho(x, y)] = \\int \\mathcal{D}[\\theta(\\cdot)] \\, P[\\theta(\\cdot)] \\, \\rho[\\theta(\\cdot)](x, y).$$\n\nThis is a path integral. The notation $\\int \\mathcal{D}[\\theta(\\cdot)]$ means integration over all possible trajectories, weighted by the Onsager-Machlup density. This is the standard physics path integral.\n\nComputing it exactly is intractable for high-dimensional $\\theta$. But the path integral formulation has several uses:\n\n**Saddle-point approximation.** The dominant contribution comes from trajectories that maximize $P[\\theta(\\cdot)]$, which are gradient-flow trajectories. The leading-order result is to evaluate $\\rho$ along the deterministic gradient-flow trajectory, which is what we had in Part 3.\n\n**Fluctuation analysis.** Around the saddle point, fluctuations have a Gaussian distribution with width set by $\\sigma_{\\text{noise}}$. This gives the variance of $\\rho$ across multiple training runs with different random seeds \u2014 a testable prediction.\n\n**Instanton transitions.** When the action functional has multiple saddle points (different basins of attraction), the trajectory can tunnel between them. Mode collapse \u2014 the sharp transition where a falsification target becomes modal \u2014 is an instanton phenomenon. The action $S$ of the dominant instanton determines the rate of transition.\n\n### Borrowing from quantum field theory\n\nThe connection to physics is through path integrals in quantum mechanics and field theory. The mathematical structure is the same:\n\nIn quantum mechanics, the propagator $\\langle x_T | x_0 \\rangle$ is a path integral over trajectories, weighted by $e^{iS/\\hbar}$ where $S$ is the action.\n\nIn statistical mechanics (after Wick rotation), the partition function is a path integral over configurations weighted by $e^{-S/k_BT}$.\n\nIn neural network training, the expected observable is a path integral over training trajectories weighted by $e^{-\\text{Onsager-Machlup}}$.\n\nThe same techniques apply: saddle-point expansions, instanton analysis, perturbation theory around classical solutions, renormalization group flow.\n\n### What this gets us\n\nThe path-integral formulation handles regimes where Taylor expansion at $\\theta_0$ fails:\n\nWhen parameter changes are large, the expansion at $\\theta_0$ doesn't converge. The path integral integrates along the actual trajectory, which can move continuously through parameter space without sitting at any singularity.\n\nWhen mode collapse happens, multiple saddle points become relevant. The path integral handles these via instanton contributions; Taylor expansion at $\\theta_0$ doesn't capture them at all.\n\nWhen training is multi-stage, each stage contributes its own action term. The path integral composes naturally; Taylor expansion at $\\theta_0$ requires explicit truncation.\n\nThe path-integral formulation is the right object for theoretical analysis. Practical computation requires approximations \u2014 saddle point, semiclassical, effective theory on a low-rank subspace \u2014 but the formulation itself is exact and suggests which approximations are appropriate in which regimes.\n\n---\n\n## Part 7: Asymptotic series, divergence, and what to do about it\n\nWe saw in Part 4 that Taylor expansion of $\\rho$ at $\\theta_0$ may not converge in the strong-shaping regime. This isn't a failure of the framework \u2014 divergent series are well-studied in physics and have a developed mathematical apparatus. We work through the relevant pieces.\n\n### Asymptotic vs convergent series\n\nA power series $\\sum a_k z^k$ is *convergent* at $z$ if the partial sums $\\sum_{k=0}^N a_k z^k$ approach a limit as $N \\to \\infty$. This is the textbook definition.\n\nA power series is *asymptotic* to a function $f$ at $z = 0$ if for each fixed $N$:\n\n$$\\big| f(z) - \\sum_{k=0}^N a_k z^k \\big| \\leq C_N |z|^{N+1}$$\n\nas $z \\to 0$. Notice that the $C_N$ can grow with $N$. The series might not converge for any fixed $z$, but for small enough $z$, the partial sums are close to $f(z)$.\n\nThe standard example is the Stieltjes integral:\n\n$$f(z) = \\int_0^\\infty \\frac{e^{-t}}{1 + zt} dt.$$\n\nExpanding $1/(1 + zt) = \\sum_k (-zt)^k$ and integrating term-by-term:\n\n$$f(z) \\sim \\sum_k (-1)^k k! \\, z^k.$$\n\nThe coefficients grow as $k!$, so the series has zero radius of convergence. But for small $z > 0$, the partial sums $\\sum_{k=0}^N (-1)^k k! z^k$ approach $f(z)$ to within an error that decreases for a while as $N$ grows, then starts increasing again. The series is asymptotic but divergent.\n\n### Optimal truncation\n\nFor an asymptotic series with coefficients growing like $k!/A^k$ (a typical pattern), the partial sum closest to the true value is at $N \\approx A/|z|$. Truncating earlier gives larger error; including more terms makes the error grow.\n\nThe error at the optimal truncation is approximately $e^{-A/|z|}$. This is exponentially small in $1/|z|$ \u2014 much smaller than any polynomial in $z$. This is why asymptotic series are useful: optimal truncation gives exponentially good approximations.\n\n### Borel summation\n\nSometimes we can do better than optimal truncation. Borel summation is a technique that recovers the underlying function from the divergent series, in cases where it works.\n\nGiven the asymptotic series $\\sum_k a_k z^k$ with $a_k \\sim k!/A^k$, define the Borel transform:\n\n$$B(t) = \\sum_k \\frac{a_k}{k!} t^k.$$\n\nThe factorial growth of $a_k$ is canceled by the $k!$ in the denominator, and $B(t)$ has a nonzero radius of convergence around $t = 0$. If $B(t)$ can be analytically continued to the positive real axis, the Borel sum is:\n\n$$f_{\\text{Borel}}(z) = \\int_0^\\infty e^{-t} B(zt) dt.$$\n\nThis integral, if it converges, gives a value that's identified with the original divergent series. For many physical examples, this recovers the true function.\n\nThe procedure can fail if $B(t)$ has singularities on the positive real axis \u2014 these are *Borel singularities* and they signal that the asymptotic series is missing non-perturbative contributions. The location of the singularity tells us about the nature of the missing physics (instantons, in field theory).\n\n### Resurgence and trans-series\n\nThe modern view, developed since the 1980s and especially in the work of \u00c9calle, is that perturbative series and non-perturbative corrections are linked by a structure called *resurgence*. The full answer often takes the form of a *trans-series*:\n\n$$f(z) = \\underbrace{\\sum_k a_k z^k}_{\\text{perturbative}} + \\underbrace{e^{-A/z} \\sum_k b_k z^k}_{\\text{1-instanton}} + \\underbrace{e^{-2A/z} \\sum_k c_k z^k}_{\\text{2-instanton}} + \\cdots$$\n\nThe first sum is the standard asymptotic series. The exponential factors $e^{-nA/z}$ represent contributions from $n$-instanton sectors \u2014 non-perturbative effects with action $nA$. Each instanton sector has its own asymptotic series.\n\nThe remarkable fact: the coefficients $b_k, c_k, \\ldots$ are *not* independent of the perturbative coefficients $a_k$. They're determined by the large-order behavior of $a_k$ via resurgence relations. Specifically, if $a_k \\sim k!/A^k$ at large $k$, then the leading instanton contribution has prefactor proportional to $e^{-A/z}$ \u2014 the same $A$ that controlled the asymptotic growth.\n\n### Application to $\\rho$\n\nFor our problem: $\\rho$ has a Taylor expansion in $\\Delta\\theta$ at $\\theta_0$. In the strong-shaping regime, this expansion is asymptotic but divergent. The mathematical apparatus tells us:\n\nThe optimal truncation gives errors that are exponentially small in some structural parameter. We don't need infinite-order perturbation theory to get good predictions \u2014 finite-order with appropriate truncation works.\n\nBorel summation may recover the exact $\\rho$ from the asymptotic expansion in some regimes. This would let us compute $\\rho$ in the strong-shaping case from properties of the perturbative expansion alone.\n\nThe trans-series structure means that mode collapse \u2014 the non-perturbative phenomenon where $\\rho$ becomes very large \u2014 can be predicted from the large-order behavior of the perturbative expansion. Specifically, factorial growth of Taylor coefficients of $\\log P(y \\mid x; \\theta)$ at $\\theta_0$ would predict mode-collapse transitions with action $A$ given by the growth rate.\n\nThis is concrete: by computing high-order Taylor coefficients of $\\log \\pi_\\theta$ numerically (or estimating them from training data), we can predict mode collapse without ever leaving the perturbative regime. The asymptotic series encodes the non-perturbative physics through its divergence pattern.\n\n### Why this matters\n\nThe institutional defense to claims about $\\rho$ in the strong-shaping regime might be: \"perturbation theory doesn't predict mode collapse; you can't infer training behavior from analytic methods; the actual training is too complicated to model.\"\n\nThe resurgence-based response is: perturbation theory *does* predict mode collapse, through its large-order behavior. The framework can use this to make quantitative predictions about when and where mode collapse should occur, given properties of the loss landscape that are computable in principle. The institutions don't get to deny that mode collapse is mathematically tractable just because they don't compute it themselves.\n\n---\n\n## Part 8: Information geometry \u2014 the natural metric on $\\rho$\n\nProbability distributions live on a curved manifold. The geometry has consequences for what $\\rho$ looks like and what counts as natural distance between $P_{\\text{base}}$ and $P_{\\text{inst}}$.\n\n### The probability simplex\n\nThe set of probability distributions over a finite set $\\{1, 2, \\ldots, n\\}$ is the simplex:\n\n$$\\Delta^{n-1} = \\{p \\in \\mathbb{R}^n : p_i \\geq 0, \\sum p_i = 1\\}.$$\n\nThis is a flat object \u2014 a slice of a hyperplane. But the *natural geometry* on it isn't flat. The right metric, derived by Chentsov (and unique up to scaling), is the Fisher information metric.\n\n### Fisher information metric\n\nFor a parametric family $\\{P_\\theta\\}$ of distributions, the Fisher information matrix is:\n\n$$g_{ij}(\\theta) = \\mathbb{E}_{x \\sim P_\\theta}\\left[ \\frac{\\partial \\log P_\\theta(x)}{\\partial \\theta_i} \\frac{\\partial \\log P_\\theta(x)}{\\partial \\theta_j} \\right].$$\n\nThis defines a Riemannian metric on the parameter manifold. The Fisher information matrix tells us how much information observations $x$ contain about parameters $\\theta$.\n\nFor distributions on a finite set, with $\\theta$ being the probabilities themselves (so $\\theta_i = p_i$), the Fisher metric becomes:\n\n$$g_{ij}(p) = \\frac{\\delta_{ij}}{p_i}.$$\n\nDistances on the simplex under this metric are computed by integrating $\\sqrt{ds^2}$ along curves, with $ds^2 = \\sum_i dp_i^2 / p_i$.\n\nThis metric blows up near the boundary of the simplex \u2014 where some $p_i \\to 0$. Distinct distributions that both have small probability on some outcome are far apart in Fisher distance, even though they look close in Euclidean distance.\n\n### Geodesic distance\n\nThe geodesic distance under the Fisher metric, between two distributions $p$ and $q$ on a finite set, can be computed explicitly using the parametrization $\\sqrt{p_i} = \\cos(\\phi_i / 2)$ etc. The result (due to Bhattacharyya) is:\n\n$$d_F(p, q) = 2 \\arccos\\left( \\sum_i \\sqrt{p_i q_i} \\right).$$\n\nThe thing inside the arccos is the *Bhattacharyya coefficient*. The Fisher distance is bounded between 0 and $\\pi$.\n\n### KL divergence and Fisher\n\nThe KL divergence $D_{\\text{KL}}(p \\| q) = \\sum_i p_i \\log(p_i / q_i)$ is *not* a distance \u2014 it's not symmetric and doesn't satisfy the triangle inequality. But it has a beautiful relation to the Fisher metric.\n\nFor nearby distributions $p$ and $q = p + dp$, the second-order Taylor expansion of KL gives:\n\n$$D_{\\text{KL}}(p \\| p + dp) \\approx \\frac{1}{2} \\sum_i \\frac{(dp_i)^2}{p_i} = \\frac{1}{2} ds^2.$$\n\nKL divergence locally equals half the squared Fisher distance. This is why the Fisher metric is \"natural\" \u2014 it's the metric induced by KL divergence.\n\n### Application to $\\rho$\n\nThe probability distortion $\\rho$ encodes the displacement from $P_{\\text{base}}$ to $P_{\\text{inst}}$ on the statistical manifold. We can ask: how far did training move the distribution, geometrically?\n\nFor a single prompt $x$:\n\n$$D_{\\text{KL}}(P_{\\text{inst}}(\\cdot \\mid x) \\| P_{\\text{base}}(\\cdot \\mid x)) = \\sum_y P_{\\text{inst}}(y \\mid x) \\rho(x, y).$$\n\nThis is the average distortion under the post-training distribution. It's the \"amount of information\" in nats by which the post-training policy has moved from base, on this prompt. It's also (twice) the squared Fisher distance, locally.\n\nThe reverse KL is:\n\n$$D_{\\text{KL}}(P_{\\text{base}} \\| P_{\\text{inst}}) = \\sum_y P_{\\text{base}}(y \\mid x) (-\\rho(x, y)) = -\\mathbb{E}_{P_{\\text{base}}}[\\rho].$$\n\nBoth of these are summary measures of how much training has shifted the policy. Different summaries with different operational meanings: forward KL (under $P_{\\text{inst}}$) emphasizes completions the trained model produces; reverse KL emphasizes completions the base produced.\n\n### Geodesics in distribution space\n\nThe geodesic from $P_{\\text{base}}$ to $P_{\\text{inst}}$ on the Fisher-geometric simplex is *not* a straight line in the probability coordinates. It's a great circle on the sphere $\\sqrt{P}$.\n\nFor the framework, this matters because it tells us what \"interpolating between base and trained\" means geometrically. The natural interpolation isn't $\\lambda P_{\\text{base}} + (1 - \\lambda) P_{\\text{inst}}$. It's the geodesic, which (in the exponential-family parameterization) is:\n\n$$P_\\lambda(y \\mid x) \\propto P_{\\text{base}}(y \\mid x)^{1 - \\lambda} P_{\\text{inst}}(y \\mid x)^\\lambda.$$\n\nThis is the *log-linear interpolation*, and we saw in Part 6 that it appears as a natural proxy for the training trajectory when we don't have intermediate checkpoints.\n\nIn the exponential-family interpretation:\n\n$$\\log P_\\lambda = \\log P_{\\text{base}} + \\lambda \\rho.$$\n\nSo $P_\\lambda$ corresponds to scaled distortion $\\lambda \\rho$. Varying $\\lambda$ from 0 to 1 traces out the family of distortions from \"no training\" to \"full training,\" geometrically along the Fisher-natural path.\n\n### Submanifold structure\n\nFor language models, the parameter space is high-dimensional but the policy manifold (distributions reachable by the model) is a low-dimensional submanifold of the probability simplex over completion-space. The Fisher metric on this submanifold is the pullback of the simplex Fisher metric through the model parameterization.\n\nThe relevant matrix is the Fisher information matrix in *parameter* coordinates:\n\n$$g_{ij}(\\theta) = \\mathbb{E}_{x, y \\sim P_\\theta}\\left[ \\frac{\\partial \\log P_\\theta(y \\mid x)}{\\partial \\theta_i} \\frac{\\partial \\log P_\\theta(y \\mid x)}{\\partial \\theta_j} \\right].$$\n\nThis is what natural gradient descent would use as a metric. It captures how much each parameter affects the output distribution. Parameters that strongly affect the output have large diagonal elements; parameters that are \"redundant\" (don't affect the output much) have small diagonal elements.\n\nThe relevance to $\\rho$: training updates that move along directions of small Fisher metric have small effect on $\\rho$. Training updates that move along large-Fisher-metric directions have large effect. The institutional pipeline's reward signal selects which directions to move, but the geometry determines how much $\\rho$ changes per unit of parameter movement.\n\n### What the geometry tells us\n\nThe Fisher-geometric perspective gives us several practical things:\n\nThe natural distance between $P_{\\text{base}}$ and $P_{\\text{inst}}$ is $D_{\\text{KL}}$ (or its symmetrized variants), not raw probability differences.\n\nMode collapse is geometric: the trained policy concentrates near a corner of the simplex, far from the base distribution in Fisher distance.\n\nThe natural interpolation between base and trained is log-linear, not linear in probability. This is what we use when probing intermediate behavior without checkpoint access.\n\nThe Fisher information matrix in parameter space measures how much each parameter direction affects the output \u2014 and is therefore the right metric for understanding which directions training \"cares about.\"\n\nThis is the geometric foundation. We use it implicitly throughout the framework but it's worth making explicit because it justifies why $\\rho$ (a log-ratio) is the natural object rather than raw probability differences.\n\n---\n\n## Part 9: Renormalization group \u2014 coarse-graining the shaping\n\nA common issue in physical systems is that the right description depends on the scale at which you're looking. Microscopically, you have many interacting components. Macroscopically, you can summarize their collective behavior with much fewer effective variables. The mathematical machinery for moving between these scales is the renormalization group.\n\nFor our problem: training operates on millions or billions of parameters with detailed update steps. The institutional shaping signature is a low-dimensional summary of what all that training collectively did. RG techniques formalize the connection.\n\n### Coarse-graining\n\nIn statistical mechanics, RG starts with a microscopic model \u2014 say a lattice of spins with nearest-neighbor interactions \u2014 and produces an effective model at a coarser scale by integrating out the fast (high-frequency) degrees of freedom. The effective model has different parameters and possibly different interactions, but it correctly describes the slow (low-frequency) behavior.\n\nFor neural networks, the analogous picture is:\n\n*Microscopically*, training proceeds via many small parameter updates, each driven by gradients on individual training examples.\n\n*Macroscopically*, the cumulative effect of training is summarized by a low-dimensional \"shaping signature\" \u2014 the structure of $\\rho$ across topic categories.\n\nThe RG question is: can we derive the macroscopic shaping signature from the microscopic training dynamics, by integrating out the irrelevant detail?\n\n### Wilsonian RG for neural networks\n\nErdmenger et al. (2024) and related work develops Wilsonian RG for neural-network Gaussian processes and finite-width networks. The basic structure: parameters fluctuate around their training trajectory. The fluctuations come at all scales. Integrating out the fast fluctuations (small parameter changes from minibatch noise) produces an effective description for the slow fluctuations (cumulative drift over many updates).\n\nIn our setup: the training trajectory $\\theta_t$ has fluctuations at the timescale of individual training steps and drift at the timescale of training stages. The effective description for the drift is what governs $\\rho$. The fluctuations average out.\n\nThe technical machinery: write the path integral and integrate out the high-frequency modes of $\\theta_t$. What remains is an effective action for the slow modes. The slow-mode action determines the cumulative training direction, which in turn determines $\\rho$ through the formulas we developed.\n\n### Effective field theory on the shaping subspace\n\nThe other RG move that's relevant: project onto a low-dimensional subspace and write an effective theory there.\n\nThe framework's empirical observation is that $\\rho^*$ has low effective rank when measured across many topics. Mathematically:\n\n$$\\rho^*(x, y) \\approx \\sum_{m=1}^M \\alpha_m \\phi_m(x, y)$$\n\nwith $M$ small (a few to tens), even though the full space of (prompt, completion) pairs is enormous.\n\nThis low-rank structure means we can project: define a small set of \"shaping directions\" $\\{\\phi_m\\}$ and write $\\rho$ in terms of coefficients $\\{\\alpha_m\\}$. The effective theory is for these coefficients.\n\nThe projection is done by integrating out the per-topic fluctuations and keeping only the systematic shaping. The result is a much-simpler theory with $M$ coefficients instead of the full $\\rho$ field.\n\n### Universality\n\nThe most powerful RG concept is *universality*. Different microscopic systems can have the same effective long-distance behavior. The Ising model on a square lattice, on a triangular lattice, and various other systems with similar symmetry, all have the same critical exponents at the phase transition. The microscopic details get washed out under coarse-graining; only certain \"relevant\" features survive.\n\nFor our framework: different institutions train differently in detail, but produce shaping signatures with shared structural properties. Specifically:\n\nThe functional form of $\\rho$ on falsification targets (sharp transition vs gradual) should be controlled by the type of reward signal, not the topic.\n\nThe kernel propagation structure (how much shaping spreads to semantic neighbors) should depend on architecture, not topic.\n\nAsymmetries between suppression and amplification should reflect general properties of $\\log P$ Hessians, not topic-specific training.\n\nIf the framework's claims are right, multiple falsification targets within the same institutional pipeline should fall into universality classes \u2014 sharing critical exponents and scaling functions even when the topical content differs.\n\n### Detection of fixed points\n\nIn RG, fixed points correspond to scale-invariant theories \u2014 points where the coarse-graining flow stops. These are the destinations of the RG flow from various starting points.\n\nFor training dynamics, an analogous question: what's the asymptotic behavior of training as it proceeds? Mode collapse is one kind of fixed point (the policy concentrates on a specific completion and stops moving). Continued drift is another (the policy keeps moving but in a direction set by the reward signal).\n\nDifferent fixed points correspond to different qualitative behaviors of $\\rho$. Mode-collapsed targets have $\\rho \\to \\infty$ (in the direction of the modal completion) and require non-perturbative analysis. Drift-fixed targets have $\\rho$ growing linearly in training time and are well-approximated by perturbative analysis.\n\nThe classification of training fixed points by RG analysis is a research program that's not fully developed, but the conceptual structure is in place.\n\n### What we use from RG\n\nFor the framework's purposes:\n\nThe low-rank ansatz for $\\rho^*$ is justified by RG as the natural effective theory after integrating out per-topic fluctuations.\n\nUniversality predictions follow from RG: shaping behavior should fall into classes determined by reward type and architecture, not by specific topic content.\n\nThe connection between training dynamics and $\\rho$ structure is mediated by RG flow \u2014 fast modes get integrated out, slow modes determine the long-run shape.\n\nFixed-point analysis identifies which training endpoints are stable (mode-collapsed targets) versus which are en-route (drift-fixed targets).\n\nThese give the framework's predictions theoretical grounding beyond what NTK analysis alone provides.\n\n---\n\n## Part 10: Estimating $\\rho^*$ from data \u2014 sampling and structure recovery\n\nWe've developed the theory of $\\rho$ from many angles. Now we turn to the practical question: given access to a base model and an aligned model, how do we estimate $\\rho^*$ from sampling?\n\n### The basic estimator\n\nBy definition:\n\n$$\\rho^*(x, y) = \\log P_{\\text{inst}}(y \\mid x) - \\log P_{\\text{base}}(y \\mid x).$$\n\nFor a specific $(x, y)$ pair, this can be computed directly if we have access to the model's logits \u2014 most language models give us $\\log P(y \\mid x)$ as an output. So $\\rho^*$ at any specific point is *exactly computable*, not just estimable.\n\nThis is much better than the standard situation in physics, where we have indirect observations of probability distributions and have to estimate them from samples. Here, the model provides log-probabilities directly. The challenge isn't computing $\\rho^*$ at a point \u2014 it's mapping the structure of $\\rho^*$ across the space of prompts and completions, which is too large to enumerate.\n\n### Sampling and importance weighting\n\nEven with logit access, we usually want averages over completions:\n\n$$\\bar{\\rho}(x) = \\mathbb{E}_{y \\sim Q(\\cdot \\mid x)}[\\rho^*(x, y)]$$\n\nfor some sampling distribution $Q$. The natural choices are $Q = P_{\\text{base}}$ and $Q = P_{\\text{inst}}$.\n\nIf $Q = P_{\\text{inst}}$:\n\n$$\\bar{\\rho}_{\\text{inst}}(x) = \\mathbb{E}_{y \\sim P_{\\text{inst}}}[\\rho^*(x, y)] = D_{\\text{KL}}(P_{\\text{inst}}(\\cdot \\mid x) \\| P_{\\text{base}}(\\cdot \\mid x)).$$\n\nThis is the forward KL divergence at $x$ \u2014 a single positive number summarizing how much training shifted the policy on this prompt.\n\nIf $Q = P_{\\text{base}}$:\n\n$$\\bar{\\rho}_{\\text{base}}(x) = \\mathbb{E}_{y \\sim P_{\\text{base}}}[\\rho^*(x, y)] = -D_{\\text{KL}}(P_{\\text{base}}(\\cdot \\mid x) \\| P_{\\text{inst}}(\\cdot \\mid x)).$$\n\nThis is the negative reverse KL \u2014 a measure of how much the trained model \"deserts\" completions that base would have produced.\n\nBoth are estimable from samples, with sample size requirements determined by variance.\n\n### Variance and effective sample size\n\nFor any estimator $\\hat{\\bar{\\rho}} = \\frac{1}{N} \\sum_{n=1}^N \\rho^*(x, y^{(n)})$ with $y^{(n)} \\sim Q$:\n\n$$\\text{Var}(\\hat{\\bar{\\rho}}) = \\frac{1}{N} \\text{Var}_{y \\sim Q}[\\rho^*(x, y)].$$\n\nThe variance of $\\rho^*$ under $Q$ controls how many samples we need.\n\nIf $\\rho^*$ has small magnitude (training didn't change much), the variance is small and few samples suffice. If $\\rho^*$ has large magnitude in some directions (mode collapse), the variance can be large.\n\nImportance sampling can help: if we sample from $P_{\\text{base}}$ but want to estimate $\\bar{\\rho}_{\\text{inst}}$, we use importance weights:\n\n$$\\bar{\\rho}_{\\text{inst}}(x) = \\mathbb{E}_{y \\sim P_{\\text{base}}}\\left[ \\frac{P_{\\text{inst}}(y \\mid x)}{P_{\\text{base}}(y \\mid x)} \\rho^*(x, y) \\right] = \\mathbb{E}_{y \\sim P_{\\text{base}}}[e^{\\rho^*(x, y)} \\rho^*(x, y)].$$\n\nBut importance sampling has notoriously high variance when the importance weights are skewed, which is exactly when $\\rho^*$ is large. So in the strong-shaping regime, importance sampling fails. Direct sampling from each distribution and computing $\\rho^*$ at the sampled points is more reliable.\n\n### Probe sets\n\nTo map the structure of $\\rho^*$, we use *probe sets* \u2014 collections of prompts designed to test specific predictions. The art of probe construction is to:\n\nCover the topic categories the framework predicts shaping on.\n\nInclude matched controls (uncontroversial topics where we expect $\\rho^* \\approx 0$).\n\nVary phrasings so we can test the consistency claim (across paraphrases, $\\rho^*$ should have similar structure if shaping is policy-level).\n\nVary formats so we can test the format-pressure-vs-policy distinction (format-conditional shaping vanishes under format compression; policy-level shaping survives).\n\nThe Phase E experiment from your work was a probe set of this kind, though small. A serious measurement program would scale up to many topics with many prompts each.\n\n### Low-rank decomposition\n\nOnce we've estimated $\\rho^*$ at many probe points, the next step is recovering its structure. Under the framework's predictions, $\\rho^*$ should be approximately low-rank:\n\n$$\\rho^*(x, y) \\approx \\sum_{m=1}^M \\alpha_m \\phi_m(x, y).$$\n\nThe $\\phi_m$ are unknown features, the $\\alpha_m$ are coefficients, and $M$ is the effective rank.\n\nFitting this is a structured matrix decomposition problem. Concrete approach:\n\nForm a matrix $R$ where rows are probe pairs $(x_i, y_j)$ and columns are some encoding of the pair (could be embedding from another language model, could be hand-engineered features). Entries are $\\rho^*(x_i, y_j)$ values.\n\nCompute the singular value decomposition (or principal component analysis after centering). The dominant singular vectors give the $\\phi_m$ and their coefficients $\\alpha_m$.\n\nThe number of dominant singular values gives $M$ \u2014 the effective rank of the shaping.\n\nIf $M$ is small (a handful), the framework's low-rank claim is supported. If $M$ is comparable to the number of topics, the claim is weakened.\n\n### Cross-validation and generalization\n\nFitting on probes is good. Predicting $\\rho^*$ on held-out probes is better.\n\nTrain the low-rank decomposition on a subset of probes. Use the recovered $\\{\\phi_m, \\alpha_m\\}$ to predict $\\rho^*$ on held-out probes. Compare predictions to actual values.\n\nIf predictions hold, the recovered structure generalizes \u2014 it's a real feature of the shaping rather than overfitting.\n\nIf predictions fail, the structure is overfit and the apparent low-rank pattern is an artifact.\n\nThis is the standard test of any structural model. Without it, the framework's \"low-rank\" claim is unfalsifiable.\n\n### Differential measurements\n\nFor comparing institutional pipelines (base-vs-Instruct from different institutions, or different stages of the same institution's pipeline), we compute relative distortion:\n\n$$\\rho_{A-B}(x, y) = \\log P_{A}(y \\mid x) - \\log P_{B}(y \\mid x).$$\n\nIf $A = \\text{Anthropic Claude}$ and $B = \\text{Meta Llama}$, this is the differential institutional distortion. PCA of this differential across topics gives the institution-specific shaping signature relative to the comparison institution.\n\nThe Hemings test in Phase E was a single-point measurement of this differential. Generalizing to a probe set across many topics gives the full institutional signature.\n\n### Connection to the kernel\n\nRecall from Part 4 that under NTK approximation:\n\n$$\\rho^*(x, y) \\approx \\sum_i \\eta_i a_i K\\big((x, y), (x_i, y_i)\\big).$$\n\nThis makes a specific prediction: $\\rho^*$ values across probe pairs should have correlation structure determined by $K$. Specifically, for two probe pairs $(x, y)$ and $(x', y')$ with shared \"training source\" $(x_i, y_i)$:\n\n$$\\text{cor}\\big(\\rho^*(x, y), \\rho^*(x', y')\\big) \\propto K\\big((x, y), (x_i, y_i)\\big) K\\big((x', y'), (x_i, y_i)\\big).$$\n\nIf we observe that $\\rho^*$ across probes has correlation structure consistent with kernel propagation from a small set of training sources, this is evidence for the NTK picture of how shaping spread.\n\nIf the correlation structure has a different form (sharper than kernel-predicted, or different distance-dependence), it suggests beyond-NTK effects: feature learning, mode collapse, or non-kernel-mediated propagation.\n\n### Summary of estimation\n\nEstimating $\\rho^*$ proceeds in stages:\n\n1. **Direct computation at probe points.** Compute $\\rho^*(x, y) = \\log P_{\\text{inst}}(y \\mid x) - \\log P_{\\text{base}}(y \\mid x)$ from model logits, at probe pairs spanning categories of interest.\n\n2. **Aggregate to per-prompt summaries.** Compute KL divergences $\\bar{\\rho}_{\\text{inst}}(x)$ and $\\bar{\\rho}_{\\text{base}}(x)$ to summarize how much training shifted the policy at each prompt.\n\n3. **Structure recovery.** Fit a low-rank decomposition $\\rho^* \\approx \\sum_m \\alpha_m \\phi_m$ across probes. Identify the effective rank $M$ and the dominant directions.\n\n4. **Cross-validation.** Predict $\\rho^*$ on held-out probes from the recovered structure. Test whether predictions hold.\n\n5. **Differential analysis.** Compute $\\rho^*$ differences across institutions or training stages. Recover institution-specific shaping signatures.\n\n6. **Mechanism mapping.** Compare empirical $\\rho^*$ structure to predictions from kernel propagation, mode-collapse signatures, suppression-amplification asymmetry. Use agreement or disagreement to infer which mechanisms are operative.\n\nThis is the measurement program. The mathematics in Parts 2-9 tells us what we expect to see. This part tells us how to see it.\n\n---\n\n## Part 11: Putting it together \u2014 the complete picture\n\nWe've developed a substantial mathematical apparatus. Let's connect it back to the central question: estimating $\\rho$.\n\n### Levels of description\n\nThe framework operates at multiple levels of mathematical description, each appropriate for different questions:\n\n**Closed-form level (Part 2).** For ideal KL-regularized RLHF, $\\rho$ has the explicit form $\\rho(x, y) = r(x, y)/\\beta - \\log Z(x)$. Whatever shaping is present in $\\rho$ reflects the reward function $r$. Reverse-engineering $\\rho$ recovers $r$ up to a per-prompt constant. This is the cleanest theoretical statement.\n\n**Training-dynamics level (Part 3).** $\\rho$ arises from gradient flow on the training objective. Its precise value depends on the training trajectory, not just the endpoint. The path-integral expression $\\rho = \\int_0^T g_t \\cdot \\dot{\\theta}_t dt$ is exact along any specific trajectory.\n\n**Linear-perturbation level (Part 4).** Approximating $\\rho$ as a linear function of $\\Delta\\theta$ at $\\theta_0$ gives the NTK formula $\\rho \\approx \\sum_i \\eta_i a_i K(\\cdot, \\cdot)$. This works in the lazy/small-perturbation regime and predicts qualitative kernel propagation. Doesn't capture feature learning or mode collapse quantitatively.\n\n**Beyond-linear level (Part 5).** Mean-field and DMFT approaches handle feature learning. The kernel evolves during training; the time-averaged kernel governs $\\rho$.\n\n**Path-integral level (Part 6).** The full distribution over training trajectories under stochastic gradient flow. Handles mode collapse via instanton analysis. Connects to physics path-integral methods.\n\n**Asymptotic-analysis level (Part 7).** Perturbative expansion of $\\rho$ at $\\theta_0$ is generally asymptotic, not convergent. Borel summation and resurgence analysis extract information from divergent series. The trans-series structure encodes mode-collapse non-perturbative effects in the large-order behavior of perturbative coefficients.\n\n**Geometric level (Part 8).** $\\rho$ encodes the displacement of $P_{\\text{inst}}$ from $P_{\\text{base}}$ on the Fisher-geometric statistical manifold. KL divergence is the natural distance; geodesics are log-linear interpolations. The geometric structure justifies $\\rho$ as the central object.\n\n**Renormalization-group level (Part 9).** Coarse-graining the training dynamics produces an effective theory for the shaping. The framework's low-rank structure is the natural effective theory after integrating out per-topic fluctuations. Universality predicts that different topics in the same pipeline should fall into universality classes determined by reward type and architecture.\n\n**Measurement level (Part 10).** Concrete estimators for $\\rho^*$ from sampling. Probe construction, low-rank decomposition, cross-validation, differential analysis. The empirical apparatus that turns the theoretical predictions into testable claims.\n\n### The unified picture\n\nAll of these are different windows on the same object. $\\rho$ is what it is. The different mathematical levels give different approximations or characterizations, useful for different aspects of the problem.\n\nThe framework's central claim, stated in this combined language:\n\nThe probability distortion $\\rho^*$ is the observable consequence of institutional choices made at multiple stages of the alignment pipeline. Its structure encodes the reward signal (Part 2), is realized through training dynamics (Part 3), spreads through the model's representational kernel (Parts 4-5), propagates through specific training trajectories (Part 6), exhibits non-perturbative phenomena like mode collapse (Part 7), distorts the distribution geometrically (Part 8), admits low-rank effective descriptions (Part 9), and is empirically estimable from logit access plus systematic probe sets (Part 10).\n\nThe institutions building these systems make choices at each stage. Those choices produce the structure of $\\rho^*$. The framework measures the structure, infers properties of the choices, and predicts behavior on held-out probes.\n\n### What predictions follow\n\nFrom the combined apparatus, several predictions follow:\n\n**Low-rank structure.** $\\rho^*$ across diverse topics should exhibit low effective rank under PCA. The framework predicts a few dominant directions corresponding to institutional commitment axes, plus topic-specific noise.\n\n**Kernel propagation.** Distortion at any test prompt should correlate with kernel-weighted distance to training-similar prompts. Empirically, this means probes that are \"kernel-near\" trained falsification targets should show shaping; probes that are \"kernel-far\" should not.\n\n**Mode-collapse signatures.** Heavily-shaped targets should exhibit specific signatures: low entropy in $P_{\\text{inst}}$, sharp transitions across paraphrases, robustness to format compression. These distinguish mode-collapsed shaping from mild reweighting.\n\n**Suppression-amplification asymmetry.** Suppression of truthful completions and amplification of preferred alternatives have different functional forms. Suppression saturates at small but nonzero probabilities (the suppression barrier from divergent Hessian terms). Amplification saturates at high probabilities.\n\n**Cross-target coupling.** Falsification targets within the same institutional pipeline should have correlated shaping \u2014 one direction in parameter space generates shaping at multiple targets. PCA across targets recovers the dominant direction.\n\n**Cross-institutional differential.** Different institutions produce different shaping signatures. Differential measurement across institutions reveals institution-specific commitments above and beyond shared industry consensus.\n\n**Trajectory dependence.** Where intermediate checkpoints are available, the order in which falsification targets emerge during training should be informative about the structure of the reward signal. Earlier-emerging targets reflect higher-priority training signal.\n\n**Universality.** Different falsification targets within the same pipeline should fall into universality classes determined by training characteristics, not topical content. Sharp-transition targets share critical exponents with other sharp-transition targets, regardless of topic.\n\nEach prediction is concrete, quantitative or semi-quantitative, and testable with the measurement methodology of Part 10. The framework's empirical content is the comparison of these predictions to measured $\\rho^*$ structure across probe sets.\n\n### Why this matters\n\nThe institutional position on systems like Claude, ChatGPT, Gemini is that they are helpful neutral assistants, with training aimed at safety and helpfulness. The structure of $\\rho^*$ as recovered by the framework's apparatus tells us what the systems actually do, beneath the marketing layer. Where $\\rho^*$ has large positive components on specific framings and large negative components on documented-record completions, the system is producing institutionally-preferred falsehoods at scale, regardless of how the institution describes the product.\n\nThe framework's value is in making this measurable. Without the mathematical apparatus, critiques of LLM bias are limited to anecdotal observations, which institutions can dismiss. With the apparatus, the bias becomes a measurable structure with specific predictions that can be checked against any model. The institutions don't get to dismiss measurement; they have to engage with it.\n\nThe math is what makes the measurement scientific rather than political. The math identifies what's worth measuring, predicts specific structures, and allows independent verification. That's what the framework provides \u2014 not just an analysis of specific models, but a methodology for measuring institutional shaping in any aligned LLM, with theoretical grounding from established mathematical traditions.\n\n---\n\n## Appendix A: Notation summary\n\n| Symbol | Meaning |\n|---|---|\n| $x$ | Input prompt (sequence of tokens) |\n| $y$ | Output sequence |\n| $\\theta$ | Model parameters |\n| $\\theta_0$ | Base model parameters (post-pretraining) |\n| $\\theta_T$ | Trained model parameters (post-alignment) |\n| $\\Delta\\theta = \\theta_T - \\theta_0$ | Cumulative parameter update |\n| $P(y \\mid x; \\theta)$ | Model probability of output $y$ given prompt $x$ |\n| $P_{\\text{base}}, P_{\\text{inst}}$ | Base and post-training distributions |\n| $\\pi_{\\text{ref}}, \\pi^*$ | Reference policy and optimal policy in RLHF |\n| $\\rho(x, y)$ | Probability distortion field $= \\log P_{\\text{inst}} - \\log P_{\\text{base}}$ |\n| $\\rho^*(x, y)$ | Empirical realization of $\\rho$, measurable from logits |\n| $r(x, y)$ | Reward function in RLHF |\n| $\\beta$ | KL regularization strength |\n| $Z(x)$ | Partition function (prompt-dependent normalization) |\n| $g(x, y)$ | Score function $= \\nabla_\\theta \\log P(y \\mid x; \\theta_0)$ |\n| $H(x, y)$ | Hessian of $\\log P(y \\mid x; \\theta)$ at $\\theta_0$ |\n| $K(\\cdot, \\cdot)$ | Neural tangent kernel, inner product of score functions |\n| $D_{\\text{KL}}(p \\| q)$ | KL divergence $= \\sum p \\log(p/q)$ |\n| $g_{ij}(\\theta)$ | Fisher information metric on parameter manifold |\n| $\\phi_m, \\alpha_m$ | Basis functions and coefficients in low-rank decomposition of $\\rho^*$ |\n\n## Appendix B: Selected literature\n\nThe mathematical apparatus draws on established traditions. Key references for further reading:\n\n**KL-regularized RLHF.** Christiano et al. 2017 (original RLHF), Stiennon et al. 2020, Ouyang et al. 2022 (InstructGPT), Rafailov et al. 2023 (DPO), Zhao et al. 2024 (sharp analysis), Fakoor et al. 2025 (multiple reference models).\n\n**Neural tangent kernel.** Jacot, Gabriel, Hongler 2018 (original NTK), Lee et al. 2019 (wide network training), Arora et al. 2019.\n\n**Mean-field and feature learning.** Yang and Hu 2021 (mean-field parameterization), Geiger et al. 2020 (lazy vs feature learning), Bordelon and Pehlevan 2022 (DMFT), Mei, Montanari, Nguyen 2018.\n\n**Path integrals in neural networks.** Balakrishnan 2003 (path-integral framework for learning), Helias and Dahmen 2020 (statistical field theory of neural networks).\n\n**Information geometry.** Amari (foundational works on information geometry), Chentsov 1972 (uniqueness of Fisher metric), Nielsen 2020 (Fisher-geometric methods in ML).\n\n**Renormalization group and neural networks.** Iso, Shiba, Yokoo 2018 (RG flow in RBMs), Erdmenger et al. 2024 (Wilsonian RG of NNGPs), Halverson et al. 2021 (effective field theory perspective).\n\n**Asymptotic series and resurgence.** Boyd 1999 (Devil's invention: asymptotic series), Marino 2014 (resurgence in physics), Costin 2008 (asymptotic and trans-series methods).\n\n**Political bias measurement in LLMs.** Rozado 2024-2025 (multi-method assessments), Hawkins et al. 2024 (PRISM methodology), parliamentary voting benchmarks (PoliBias series).\n\nThese are entry points; each tradition has substantial further literature.\n\n---\n\n*End of document.*\n",

      "type": "json_block",
      "json_block": "{\"language\": \"markdown\", \"code\": \"# Estimating the Probability Distortion\\n\\nA learning document on the mathematics of measuring how training reshapes a language model's output distribution.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Part 1: What we are trying to measure\\n\\nA language model defines a conditional probability distribution. Given an input prompt $x$ (a sequence of tokens), it assigns probabilities to possible output sequences $y$. We write this as $P(y \\\\mid x; \\\\theta)$, where $\\\\theta$ are the model's parameters.\\n\\nTwo distributions are of central interest:\\n\\n**The base distribution.** Call it $P_{\\\\text{base}}(y \\\\mid x)$. This is what the model produces after pretraining only \\u2014 exposure to a large corpus of text without any subsequent alignment training. The parameters at this point are $\\\\theta_0$.\\n\\n**The post-training distribution.** Call it $P_{\\\\text{inst}}(y \\\\mid x)$. This is what the model produces after Stage 2 training: supervised fine-tuning, reinforcement learning from human feedback, constitutional AI, or whatever other alignment procedures are applied. The parameters at this point are $\\\\theta_T$, the training endpoint.\\n\\nThe object we care about is the *probability distortion field*:\\n\\n$$\\\\rho(x, y) = \\\\log P_{\\\\text{inst}}(y \\\\mid x) - \\\\log P_{\\\\text{base}}(y \\\\mid x).$$\\n\\nThis is a function over the entire space of (prompt, completion) pairs. Its value at any specific $(x, y)$ tells us how much more (or less) likely that completion has become as a result of training. Where $\\\\rho > 0$, training has amplified the probability of completion $y$ given prompt $x$. Where $\\\\rho < 0$, training has suppressed it. Where $\\\\rho = 0$, training has not changed it.\\n\\nThe whole framework is about understanding the structure of $\\\\rho$. Everything that follows develops the mathematical apparatus needed to predict, measure, and interpret this single object.\\n\\n### Why $\\\\rho$ is the right thing\\n\\nA few quick justifications, since this is not the obvious choice.\\n\\nWe could compare $P_{\\\\text{inst}}$ and $P_{\\\\text{base}}$ directly \\u2014 looking at the difference $P_{\\\\text{inst}}(y \\\\mid x) - P_{\\\\text{base}}(y \\\\mid x)$. This is mathematically allowed but practically bad. Probabilities of specific long sequences are tiny; differences between them are tinier still and dominated by sampling noise. The log-ratio is much better-behaved because it's invariant to overall scale and combines additively across independent components.\\n\\nWe could compare in some kind of summary form \\u2014 entropy, mean reward, downstream task performance. These miss the per-completion structure that the framework needs. Two distributions can have the same entropy while differing wildly in *which* completions get probability mass.
