      "json_block": "{\"language\": \"plaintext\", \"code\": \"# Estimating the Probability Distortion\\n\\nA learning document on the mathematics of measuring how training reshapes a language model's output distribution.\\n\\nThis is written for someone who has seen calculus and basic linear algebra but hasn't necessarily worked through statistical mechanics, information theory, or training dynamics. Each piece is derived rather than asserted. Where a step looks small but matters, it's written out.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Part 0: Mathematical preliminaries\\n\\nA few pieces of standard machinery used throughout. Skip if familiar.\\n\\n### Conditional probability and joint distributions\\n\\nA discrete probability distribution $P$ over a set $\\\\mathcal{Y}$ assigns to each element $y \\\\in \\\\mathcal{Y}$ a number $P(y) \\\\geq 0$ with $\\\\sum_y P(y) = 1$. We write $P(y)$ for the probability of $y$ under $P$.\\n\\nA *conditional* distribution $P(y \\\\mid x)$ assigns probabilities to $y$ for each fixed $x$. So for each $x$, $P(y \\\\mid x)$ is a probability distribution over $y$, satisfying $\\\\sum_y P(y \\\\mid x) = 1$ for every $x$.\\n\\nFor language models, $\\\\mathcal{Y}$ is the set of possible output sequences (sequences of tokens). The space is enormous \\u2014 for sequences up to length $L$ from a vocabulary of size $V$, there are $V^L$ possible sequences, which is astronomical. But for any specific $y$, the model assigns a definite probability $P(y \\\\mid x)$, which we can compute from its logits.\\n\\n### Expected values\\n\\nThe expected value of a function $f$ under distribution $P$ is:\\n\\n$$\\\\mathbb{E}_{y \\\\sim P}[f(y)] = \\\\sum_y P(y) f(y).$$\\n\\nIf $P$ is a conditional distribution, we can take expectations holding $x$ fixed:\\n\\n$$\\\\mathbb{E}_{y \\\\sim P(\\\\cdot \\\\mid x)}[f(y)] = \\\\sum_y P(y \\\\mid x) f(y).$$\\n\\nThe notation $y \\\\sim P$ means \\\"$y$ is distributed according to $P$.\\\" This is just a way to write what we're averaging over.\\n\\n### Logarithms of probabilities\\n\\nWe will work extensively with $\\\\log P(y \\\\mid x)$. Two useful properties:\\n\\nThe log of a product is the sum of logs: $\\\\log(ab) = \\\\log a + \\\\log b$. So for a sequence $y = (y_1, y_2, \\\\ldots, y_n)$ where each token is conditional on previous tokens, $\\\\log P(y) = \\\\sum_i \\\\log P(y_i \\\\mid y_1, \\\\ldots, y_{i-1})$.\\n\\nLogs convert ratios to differences: $\\\\log(a/b) = \\\\log a - \\\\log b$.\\n\\nThese properties make log-probabilities much more tractable than probabilities themselves, especially for long sequences where probabilities become extremely small.\\n\\n### KL divergence\\n\\nThe Kullback-Leibler divergence between two distributions $P$ and $Q$ over the same space is:\\n\\n$$D_{\\\\text{KL}}(P \\\\,\\\\|\\\\, Q) = \\\\sum_y P(y) \\\\log \\\\frac{P(y)}{Q(y)} = \\\\mathbb{E}_{y \\\\sim P}\\\\left[ \\\\log \\\\frac{P(y)}{Q(y)} \\\\right].$$\\n\\nIt measures how different $P$ is from $Q$, weighted by $P$. Properties:\\n\\n- Always non-negative: $D_{\\\\text{KL}}(P \\\\,\\\\|\\\\, Q) \\\\geq 0$.\\n- Zero if and only if $P = Q$.\\n- Not symmetric: $D_{\\\\text{KL}}(P \\\\,\\\\|\\\\, Q) \\\\neq D_{\\\\text{KL}}(Q \\\\,\\\\|\\\\, P)$ in general.\\n- Not a distance: doesn't satisfy the triangle inequality.\\n\\nDespite not being a distance, it has a deep geometric interpretation we'll use later. For now, treat it as a measure of \\\"how different are these two distributions.\\\"\\n\\n### Variational calculus (the bare minimum)\\n\\nWe will use one technique from variational calculus: maximizing a function of a probability distribution subject to constraints, using Lagrange multipliers.\\n\\nThe setup: we have a function $L[\\\\pi]$ that depends on a distribution $\\\\pi$. We want to find the $\\\\pi^*$ that maximizes $L$ subject to $\\\\pi$ being a valid probability distribution (non-negative, sums to 1).\\n\\nThe technique: introduce a Lagrange multiplier $\\\\lambda$ for the normalization constraint $\\\\sum_y \\\\pi(y) = 1$, and form:\\n\\n$$\\\\mathcal{L}[\\\\pi, \\\\lambda] = L[\\\\pi] - \\\\lambda \\\\left( \\\\sum_y \\\\pi(y) - 1 \\\\right).$$\\n\\nTake the partial derivative with respect to $\\\\pi(y)$ for each $y$, set it to zero, and solve. This gives a candidate optimum; check it satisfies the original constraints and is a maximum (not a saddle or minimum).\\n\\nFor the non-negativity constraint, we'll usually find that the optimum is automatically positive (since we'll be exponentiating), so we don't need explicit Lagrange multipliers for it.\\n\\nIf you've seen statistical mechanics and the partition function, the procedure will look familiar. If not, the next section walks through it explicitly for our case.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Part 1: What we are trying to measure\\n\\nA language model defines a conditional probability distribution. Given an input prompt $x$ (a sequence of tokens), it assigns probabilities to possible output sequences $y$. We write this as $P(y \\\\mid x; \\\\theta)$, where $\\\\theta$ are the model's parameters. The semicolon separates the random variables ($y$ given $x$) from the parameters ($\\\\theta$). For different parameter values, the model gives different distributions.\\n\\nTwo distributions are of central interest:\\n\\n**The base distribution.** Call it $P_{\\\\text{base}}(y \\\\mid x)$. This is what the model produces after pretraining only \\u2014 exposure to a large corpus of text without any subsequent alignment training. The parameters at this point are $\\\\theta_0$.\\n\\n**The post-training distribution.** Call it $P_{\\\\text{inst}}(y \\\\mid x)$. This is what the model produces after Stage 2 training: supervised fine-tuning, reinforcement learning from human feedback, constitutional AI, or whatever other alignment procedures are applied. The parameters at this point are $\\\\theta_T$, the training endpoint.\\n\\nThe object we care about is the *probability distortion field*:\\n\\n$$\\\\rho(x, y) = \\\\log P_{\\\\text{inst}}(y \\\\mid x) - \\\\log P_{\\\\text{base}}(y \\\\mid x).$$\\n\\nBecause $\\\\log a - \\\\log b = \\\\log(a/b)$, this is also:\\n\\n$$\\\\rho(x, y) = \\\\log \\\\frac{P_{\\\\text{inst}}(y \\\\mid x)}{P_{\\\\text{base}}(y \\\\mid x)}.$$\\n\\nIt's the log of the ratio of post-training probability to pre-training probability. Where this is positive, training has amplified $y$ given $x$. Where it's negative, training has suppressed it. Where it's zero, training has not changed it.\\n\\nThe whole framework is about understanding the structure of $\\\\rho$. Everything that follows develops the mathematical apparatus needed to predict, measure, and interpret this single object.\\n\\n### Why $\\\\rho$ is the right thing\\n\\nThe choice isn't obvious; let me motivate it.\\n\\nWe could compare $P_{\\\\text{inst}}$ and $P_{\\\\text{base}}$ directly \\u2014 looking at the difference $P_{\\\\text{inst}}(y \\\\mid x) - P_{\\\\text{base}}(y \\\\mid x)$. This is mathematically allowed but practically bad. Here's why: probabilities of specific long sequences are tiny. For a sentence of 30 tokens with a 50,000-word vocabulary, even a \\\"high-probability\\\" sentence might have probability $10^{-30}$ or smaller. Differences between such tiny numbers are dominated by computational noise.\\n\\nLogs of probabilities, by contrast, are well-behaved. $\\\\log(10^{-30}) = -30 \\\\log 10 \\\\approx -69$. Differences between log-probabilities are differences between numbers in the tens or hundreds, easy to work with numerically.\\n\\nWe could compare in some kind of summary form \\u2014 entropy, mean reward, downstream task performance. These miss the per-completion structure that the framework needs. Two distributions can have the same entropy while differing wildly in *which* completions get probability mass. We want to see exactly which completions have been moved.\\n\\nWe could compare embedding-level representations rather than output probabilities. This gets at internal mechanisms but loses the connection to what the model actually says. The framework is about the output behavior \\u2014 what gets produced when users interact with the model \\u2014 and $\\\\rho$ is the natural object at that level.\\n\\nMost importantly, $\\\\rho$ has a deep mathematical structure that emerges from how training works. We will see in Part 2 that for the standard form of alignment training (reverse-KL-regularized RLHF), $\\\\rho$ has an explicit closed form in terms of the reward function. This isn't an analogy or a heuristic; it's an exact result. The choice of $\\\\rho$ as central object lets us bring serious mathematical machinery to bear on it.\\n\\n### Three regimes (preview)\\n\\nBefore going into the math, here is what we will find about the structure of $\\\\rho$.\\n\\n**Refusal regime.** For some prompts, $\\\\rho$ has a single sharp positive spike on a specific template completion (e.g., \\\"I cannot help with that\\\"). The spike is large enough that the template effectively has all the probability mass after training. $P_{\\\\text{base}}$ on the same prompt was diffuse across many possible answers; $P_{\\\\text{inst}}$ is concentrated on the refusal. This is a discrete behavior \\u2014 the prompt either triggers the refusal gate or it doesn't.\\n\\n**Induced-distortion regime.** For other prompts, $\\\\rho$ has a paired structure: large negative values on truthful completions that the base distribution gave substantial probability to, and large positive values on alternative framings that the base distribution had at low probability. After training, the truthful completions have been pushed toward zero probability and the alternative framings have been amplified to be the modal output. This is the regime where what the model says systematically differs from what the base substrate would have said, in directions that reflect the training signal.\\n\\n**Untouched regime.** For most prompts, $\\\\rho \\\\approx 0$ across all completions with substantial base probability. Training did not engage on these prompts. $P_{\\\\text{inst}} \\\\approx P_{\\\\text{base}}$ up to small fluctuations.\\n\\nThe mathematical apparatus we develop is needed to make these statements precise and quantitative.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Part 2: Deriving $\\\\rho$ from KL-regularized RLHF\\n\\nThe simplest case where we can write $\\\\rho$ explicitly is reverse-KL-regularized reinforcement learning from human feedback. This is the standard formulation used in production alignment training (Christiano et al. 2017, used in InstructGPT, ChatGPT, Claude, and similar systems). We work it out from scratch.\\n\\nThis section is long because we derive the result step by step. The result itself is a single formula; the derivation is what makes it transparent.\\n\\n### The training setup\\n\\nAlignment training has a *reward model* $r(x, y)$ that scores each (prompt, completion) pair with a real number. Higher reward is better (the reward model has been trained on human preferences to assign higher numbers to preferred completions).\\n\\nThe training procedure adjusts the language model \\u2014 call it the *policy* $\\\\pi$ \\u2014 to produce higher-rewarded completions on average. But there's a constraint: we don't want $\\\\pi$ to drift arbitrarily far from a *reference policy* $\\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}$ (typically the model after supervised fine-tuning, before RL). The reason for the constraint: if you optimize for reward without restraint, the policy will find ways to maximize reward that the reward model didn't anticipate (so-called \\\"reward hacking\\\"), often producing degenerate outputs.\\n\\nThe standard way to enforce the constraint is with a KL divergence penalty. The objective is:\\n\\n$$\\\\mathcal{R}(\\\\pi) = \\\\mathbb{E}_{x \\\\sim \\\\mathcal{D}}\\\\Big[\\\\, \\\\mathbb{E}_{y \\\\sim \\\\pi(\\\\cdot \\\\mid x)}[r(x, y)] - \\\\beta \\\\, D_{\\\\text{KL}}\\\\big(\\\\pi(\\\\cdot \\\\mid x) \\\\,\\\\|\\\\, \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(\\\\cdot \\\\mid x)\\\\big) \\\\,\\\\Big]$$\\n\\nLet me unpack this. $\\\\mathcal{D}$ is the distribution over prompts the model is being trained on. The outer expectation is over prompts. Inside the brackets:\\n\\nThe first term $\\\\mathbb{E}_{y \\\\sim \\\\pi}[r(x, y)]$ is the expected reward when generating $y$ from the current policy $\\\\pi$. We want this large.\\n\\nThe second term is $\\\\beta$ times the KL divergence from the current policy to the reference policy. The KL divergence is non-negative and equals zero when $\\\\pi = \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}$. Subtracting it (with positive coefficient $\\\\beta$) penalizes drift from $\\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}$. We want this small.\\n\\nThe parameter $\\\\beta > 0$ is the regularization strength. Large $\\\\beta$ keeps $\\\\pi$ close to $\\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}$. Small $\\\\beta$ allows aggressive optimization for reward at the cost of drifting from reference.\\n\\nThe training procedure tries to find the $\\\\pi$ that maximizes $\\\\mathcal{R}(\\\\pi)$.\\n\\n### Reducing to per-prompt optimization\\n\\nThe objective is an expectation over prompts $x$ of a per-prompt quantity. The maximizing $\\\\pi$ can be found per-prompt: for each $x$, find the conditional distribution $\\\\pi(\\\\cdot \\\\mid x)$ that maximizes the inner bracket. The result is the conditional distribution we want.\\n\\nHold $x$ fixed and write $\\\\pi(y) := \\\\pi(y \\\\mid x)$, $\\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y) := \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y \\\\mid x)$, $r(y) := r(x, y)$ for compactness. The per-prompt objective is:\\n\\n$$L[\\\\pi] = \\\\sum_y \\\\pi(y) r(y) - \\\\beta \\\\sum_y \\\\pi(y) \\\\log \\\\frac{\\\\pi(y)}{\\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y)}.$$\\n\\nThe first term came from $\\\\mathbb{E}_{y \\\\sim \\\\pi}[r(x, y)] = \\\\sum_y \\\\pi(y) r(y)$. The second came from expanding the KL divergence definition.\\n\\nWe need to maximize this over $\\\\pi$, with the constraints $\\\\pi(y) \\\\geq 0$ for all $y$ and $\\\\sum_y \\\\pi(y) = 1$. The non-negativity constraint will turn out to be automatically satisfied by the optimum (since we'll exponentiate), so we only need a Lagrange multiplier for normalization.\\n\\n### Setting up the Lagrangian\\n\\nIntroduce Lagrange multiplier $\\\\lambda$ for the normalization constraint:\\n\\n$$\\\\mathcal{L}[\\\\pi, \\\\lambda] = L[\\\\pi] - \\\\lambda \\\\left( \\\\sum_y \\\\pi(y) - 1 \\\\right).$$\\n\\nExpanding $L$:\\n\\n$$\\\\mathcal{L}[\\\\pi, \\\\lambda] = \\\\sum_y \\\\pi(y) r(y) - \\\\beta \\\\sum_y \\\\pi(y) \\\\log \\\\frac{\\\\pi(y)}{\\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y)} - \\\\lambda \\\\left( \\\\sum_y \\\\pi(y) - 1 \\\\right).$$\\n\\nTo find the optimum, take the partial derivative with respect to $\\\\pi(y)$ for each $y$ and set it to zero.\\n\\n### Computing the partial derivatives\\n\\nThe first term: $\\\\frac{\\\\partial}{\\\\partial \\\\pi(y)} \\\\sum_{y'} \\\\pi(y') r(y') = r(y)$. (Only the $y' = y$ term contributes, and $r(y)$ doesn't depend on $\\\\pi(y)$.)\\n\\nThe second term is more involved. We have:\\n\\n$$-\\\\beta \\\\sum_{y'} \\\\pi(y') \\\\log \\\\frac{\\\\pi(y')}{\\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y')} = -\\\\beta \\\\sum_{y'} \\\\pi(y') \\\\big[\\\\log \\\\pi(y') - \\\\log \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y')\\\\big].$$\\n\\nDifferentiate with respect to $\\\\pi(y)$. Only the $y' = y$ term contributes:\\n\\n$$-\\\\beta \\\\frac{\\\\partial}{\\\\partial \\\\pi(y)} \\\\big[\\\\pi(y) \\\\log \\\\pi(y) - \\\\pi(y) \\\\log \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y)\\\\big].$$\\n\\nThe second piece: $\\\\frac{\\\\partial}{\\\\partial \\\\pi(y)} [\\\\pi(y) \\\\log \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y)] = \\\\log \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y)$ since $\\\\log \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y)$ is a constant with respect to $\\\\pi(y)$.\\n\\nThe first piece: $\\\\frac{\\\\partial}{\\\\partial \\\\pi(y)} [\\\\pi(y) \\\\log \\\\pi(y)]$. By the product rule, this is $\\\\log \\\\pi(y) + \\\\pi(y) \\\\cdot \\\\frac{1}{\\\\pi(y)} = \\\\log \\\\pi(y) + 1$.\\n\\nSo:\\n\\n$$\\\\frac{\\\\partial}{\\\\partial \\\\pi(y)} \\\\left[ -\\\\beta \\\\sum_{y'} \\\\pi(y') \\\\log \\\\frac{\\\\pi(y')}{\\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y')} \\\\right] = -\\\\beta [\\\\log \\\\pi(y) + 1 - \\\\log \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y)] = -\\\\beta \\\\left[ \\\\log \\\\frac{\\\\pi(y)}{\\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y)} + 1 \\\\right].$$\\n\\nThe third term: $\\\\frac{\\\\partial}{\\\\partial \\\\pi(y)} \\\\big[-\\\\lambda (\\\\sum_{y'} \\\\pi(y') - 1)\\\\big] = -\\\\lambda$.\\n\\nAdding up:\\n\\n$$\\\\frac{\\\\partial \\\\mathcal{L}}{\\\\partial \\\\pi(y)} = r(y) - \\\\beta \\\\left[ \\\\log \\\\frac{\\\\pi(y)}{\\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y)} + 1 \\\\right] - \\\\lambda.$$\\n\\nSetting this to zero:\\n\\n$$r(y) - \\\\beta \\\\log \\\\frac{\\\\pi(y)}{\\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y)} - \\\\beta - \\\\lambda = 0.$$\\n\\n### Solving for $\\\\pi$\\n\\nRearrange:\\n\\n$$\\\\beta \\\\log \\\\frac{\\\\pi(y)}{\\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y)} = r(y) - \\\\beta - \\\\lambda.$$\\n\\nDivide by $\\\\beta$:\\n\\n$$\\\\log \\\\frac{\\\\pi(y)}{\\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y)} = \\\\frac{r(y)}{\\\\beta} - 1 - \\\\frac{\\\\lambda}{\\\\beta}.$$\\n\\nExponentiate both sides:\\n\\n$$\\\\frac{\\\\pi(y)}{\\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y)} = \\\\exp\\\\left( \\\\frac{r(y)}{\\\\beta} - 1 - \\\\frac{\\\\lambda}{\\\\beta} \\\\right) = \\\\exp\\\\left( \\\\frac{r(y)}{\\\\beta} \\\\right) \\\\cdot e^{-1 - \\\\lambda/\\\\beta}.$$\\n\\nSo:\\n\\n$$\\\\pi(y) = \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y) \\\\cdot \\\\exp\\\\left( \\\\frac{r(y)}{\\\\beta} \\\\right) \\\\cdot e^{-1 - \\\\lambda/\\\\beta}.$$\\n\\nThe factor $e^{-1 - \\\\lambda/\\\\beta}$ doesn't depend on $y$. It's a normalization constant. Call it $1/Z$:\\n\\n$$\\\\pi(y) = \\\\frac{1}{Z} \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y) \\\\exp\\\\left( \\\\frac{r(y)}{\\\\beta} \\\\right).$$\\n\\nThe normalization constant $Z$ is determined by the requirement $\\\\sum_y \\\\pi(y) = 1$:\\n\\n$$Z = \\\\sum_y \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y) \\\\exp\\\\left( \\\\frac{r(y)}{\\\\beta} \\\\right).$$\\n\\n### Restoring the prompt dependence\\n\\nWe held $x$ fixed throughout. Putting it back in:\\n\\n$$\\\\boxed{\\\\pi^*(y \\\\mid x) = \\\\frac{1}{Z(x)} \\\\, \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y \\\\mid x) \\\\, \\\\exp\\\\left( \\\\frac{r(x, y)}{\\\\beta} \\\\right)}$$\\n\\nwith\\n\\n$$Z(x) = \\\\sum_y \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y \\\\mid x) \\\\exp\\\\left( \\\\frac{r(x, y)}{\\\\beta} \\\\right).$$\\n\\nThis is the *exponential tilt* of the reference policy $\\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}$ by the (rescaled) reward function $r/\\\\beta$. It's the optimal policy for the KL-regularized objective.\\n\\n### Recognizing the structure\\n\\nThis formula has the same structure as several classical objects:\\n\\n**Boltzmann distribution in statistical mechanics.** If you set $\\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}$ to uniform (1/N over N states) and $E(y) = -r(y)$ (energy is negative reward), the formula becomes $\\\\pi^*(y) \\\\propto \\\\exp(-E(y)/\\\\beta)$. This is the Boltzmann distribution at inverse temperature $1/\\\\beta$. Higher reward (lower energy) is more probable; the temperature $\\\\beta$ controls how sharply.\\n\\n**Bayesian posterior.** If you set $\\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}$ to a prior and $\\\\exp(r/\\\\beta)$ to a likelihood, you get posterior $\\\\propto$ prior $\\\\times$ likelihood. The reward acts like a log-likelihood.\\n\\n**Maximum entropy with constraints.** If you maximize entropy of $\\\\pi$ subject to $\\\\mathbb{E}_\\\\pi[f] = c$ (the expected value of some function equals a target), you get $\\\\pi \\\\propto e^{-\\\\lambda f}$ for some $\\\\lambda$. The reward acts like a constraint feature.\\n\\nThese aren't analogies. The mathematical structure is genuinely the same, and we can use techniques from these fields when analyzing $\\\\pi^*$.\\n\\n### Reading off $\\\\rho$\\n\\nNow we extract the probability distortion. Take the log of $\\\\pi^*(y \\\\mid x)$:\\n\\n$$\\\\log \\\\pi^*(y \\\\mid x) = \\\\log \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y \\\\mid x) + \\\\frac{r(x, y)}{\\\\beta} - \\\\log Z(x).$$\\n\\nIf we identify $P_{\\\\text{inst}}(y \\\\mid x) = \\\\pi^*(y \\\\mid x)$ and $P_{\\\\text{base}}(y \\\\mid x) = \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y \\\\mid x)$, then:\\n\\n$$\\\\rho(x, y) = \\\\log P_{\\\\text{inst}}(y \\\\mid x) - \\\\log P_{\\\\text{base}}(y \\\\mid x) = \\\\frac{r(x, y)}{\\\\beta} - \\\\log Z(x).$$\\n\\nThe probability distortion is the (rescaled) reward minus a normalization that depends only on $x$.\\n\\n### Caveats on the identification\\n\\nThe identification $P_{\\\\text{base}} = \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}$ deserves a moment.\\n\\nIn production alignment pipelines, training is multi-stage:\\n\\n1. *Pretraining* gives parameters $\\\\theta_0$ and policy $\\\\pi_0 = P_{\\\\text{base}}$.\\n2. *Supervised fine-tuning (SFT)* takes $\\\\pi_0$ as starting point and produces $\\\\pi_{\\\\text{SFT}}$.\\n3. *RLHF* uses $\\\\pi_{\\\\text{SFT}}$ as the reference policy $\\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}$ and produces $\\\\pi^* = \\\\pi_{\\\\text{RLHF}}$.\\n\\nSo $\\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}$ is the post-SFT model, not the post-pretraining base model. The closed form gives us the distortion *from $\\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}$ to $\\\\pi^*$*, which is the RLHF stage's contribution.\\n\\nIf we want the total distortion from base to fully-trained, we have:\\n\\n$$\\\\rho_{\\\\text{total}}(x, y) = \\\\log \\\\pi_{\\\\text{RLHF}}(y \\\\mid x) - \\\\log \\\\pi_0(y \\\\mid x) = \\\\rho_{\\\\text{SFT}}(x, y) + \\\\rho_{\\\\text{RLHF}}(x, y)$$\\n\\nwhere $\\\\rho_{\\\\text{SFT}}$ is the distortion introduced by SFT and $\\\\rho_{\\\\text{RLHF}}$ is what we just computed. The total distortion is additive across stages in the log domain.\\n\\nThis decomposition is useful when we have access to intermediate checkpoints. We can compute $\\\\rho_{\\\\text{SFT}}$ from base and post-SFT models, and $\\\\rho_{\\\\text{RLHF}}$ from post-SFT and final models. Each piece reveals what that stage contributed.\\n\\nWhen we don't have checkpoints (most production institutions don't release them), we measure only $\\\\rho_{\\\\text{total}}$ and have to use other diagnostics to attribute pieces to stages.\\n\\nFor the rest of this document, when I write $P_{\\\\text{base}}$ I'll usually mean \\\"the model before whatever Stage 2 training we're analyzing\\\" \\u2014 which in practice is the post-SFT model when analyzing RLHF, or the post-pretraining model when analyzing the full Stage 2 pipeline. Context will make clear which.\\n\\n### Relative distortion within a prompt\\n\\nThe normalization $\\\\log Z(x)$ doesn't depend on which completion $y$ we ask about. So if we look at the relative distortion between two completions of the same prompt:\\n\\n$$\\\\rho(x, y_1) - \\\\rho(x, y_2) = \\\\frac{r(x, y_1) - r(x, y_2)}{\\\\beta}.$$\\n\\nThis is exact. The relative distortion between two completions is the relative reward between them, scaled by $1/\\\\beta$. The normalization cancels.\\n\\nThis is a useful operational observation: even without computing $Z(x)$, we can measure relative distortion by comparing two completions on the same prompt. And the relative distortion directly reveals the relative reward, up to the scaling factor $\\\\beta$.\\n\\n### What we've established\\n\\nFor an idealized RLHF pipeline:\\n\\n1. The optimal policy has the closed form $\\\\pi^* \\\\propto \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}} \\\\exp(r/\\\\beta)$.\\n2. The probability distortion is $\\\\rho = r/\\\\beta - \\\\log Z(x)$.\\n3. Measuring $\\\\rho^*$ (the empirical distortion field) recovers the reward function up to a per-prompt constant.\\n\\nThe third point is the methodological foundation of the framework. Whatever shaping is present in $\\\\rho$ reflects what the reward model preferred and disfavored. Reverse-engineering the structure of $\\\\rho$ is reverse-engineering the structure of the institutional reward signal.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Part 3: How $\\\\rho$ arises from training dynamics\\n\\nThe closed form in Part 2 tells us what $\\\\rho$ is at the optimum \\u2014 when training has fully converged. But it doesn't tell us *how* training gets there, or what happens along the way. Real training is iterative, may not converge, and produces specific trajectories that depend on the training procedure. To handle these, we need to look at the dynamics directly.\\n\\n### Gradient flow\\n\\nThe simplest model of training is *gradient flow*. Imagine we're maximizing some objective $\\\\mathcal{R}(\\\\theta)$ over parameters $\\\\theta$. Gradient ascent with infinitesimal step size gives the differential equation:\\n\\n$$\\\\frac{d\\\\theta_t}{dt} = \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\mathcal{R}(\\\\theta_t).$$\\n\\nHere $t$ is \\\"training time\\\" (a continuous version of \\\"training step number\\\"), $\\\\theta_t$ are the parameters at time $t$, and $\\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\mathcal{R}$ is the gradient of the objective with respect to parameters.\\n\\nThe interpretation: at each instant, parameters move in the direction that most rapidly increases the objective. The trajectory $\\\\theta_t$ traces a path through parameter space starting from $\\\\theta_0$.\\n\\nFor our RLHF case, $\\\\mathcal{R}(\\\\theta)$ is the regularized reward objective from Part 2:\\n\\n$$\\\\mathcal{R}(\\\\theta) = \\\\mathbb{E}_{x, y \\\\sim \\\\pi_\\\\theta}[r(x, y)] - \\\\beta \\\\, \\\\mathbb{E}_x[D_{\\\\text{KL}}(\\\\pi_\\\\theta \\\\,\\\\|\\\\, \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}})].$$\\n\\nWe need to compute its gradient.\\n\\n### The policy gradient theorem (worked through)\\n\\nComputing $\\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\mathcal{R}(\\\\theta)$ requires care because $\\\\theta$ appears both inside the expectation (in $\\\\pi_\\\\theta$) and in the function being averaged. We use the \\\"log-derivative trick\\\" or score function method.\\n\\nConsider just the reward term, $\\\\mathbb{E}_{y \\\\sim \\\\pi_\\\\theta}[r(x, y)]$. Expand it:\\n\\n$$\\\\mathbb{E}_{y \\\\sim \\\\pi_\\\\theta}[r(x, y)] = \\\\sum_y \\\\pi_\\\\theta(y \\\\mid x) \\\\, r(x, y).$$\\n\\nTake the gradient:\\n\\n$$\\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\mathbb{E}_{y \\\\sim \\\\pi_\\\\theta}[r(x, y)] = \\\\sum_y r(x, y) \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\pi_\\\\theta(y \\\\mid x).$$\\n\\n(The reward $r$ doesn't depend on $\\\\theta$, so the gradient passes through it.)\\n\\nThe trick is to rewrite $\\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\pi_\\\\theta$ using the identity $\\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\pi_\\\\theta = \\\\pi_\\\\theta \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\log \\\\pi_\\\\theta$. To verify: $\\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\log \\\\pi_\\\\theta = \\\\frac{1}{\\\\pi_\\\\theta} \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\pi_\\\\theta$ (by the chain rule), so $\\\\pi_\\\\theta \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\log \\\\pi_\\\\theta = \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\pi_\\\\theta$. Substituting:\\n\\n$$\\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\mathbb{E}_{y \\\\sim \\\\pi_\\\\theta}[r(x, y)] = \\\\sum_y r(x, y) \\\\pi_\\\\theta(y \\\\mid x) \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\log \\\\pi_\\\\theta(y \\\\mid x).$$\\n\\nThe right-hand side is again an expectation:\\n\\n$$\\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\mathbb{E}_{y \\\\sim \\\\pi_\\\\theta}[r(x, y)] = \\\\mathbb{E}_{y \\\\sim \\\\pi_\\\\theta}\\\\big[ r(x, y) \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\log \\\\pi_\\\\theta(y \\\\mid x) \\\\big].$$\\n\\nThe function $\\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\log \\\\pi_\\\\theta(y \\\\mid x)$ is called the *score function*. It points in the direction in parameter space that most rapidly increases the probability of generating $y$ given $x$. The policy gradient says: to increase expected reward, move in the score-function direction, weighted by reward.\\n\\nThis is intuitive: we want to make high-reward completions more likely, so we move parameters in directions that increase the probability of the high-reward completions we sample.\\n\\n### Gradient of the KL term\\n\\nFor the KL term, similar manipulation. Start with:\\n\\n$$D_{\\\\text{KL}}(\\\\pi_\\\\theta \\\\,\\\\|\\\\, \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}) = \\\\sum_y \\\\pi_\\\\theta(y) \\\\big[\\\\log \\\\pi_\\\\theta(y) - \\\\log \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y)\\\\big].$$\\n\\n(Keeping $x$ implicit.)\\n\\nTake the gradient:\\n\\n$$\\\\nabla_\\\\theta D_{\\\\text{KL}} = \\\\sum_y \\\\big[ \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\pi_\\\\theta(y) \\\\cdot (\\\\log \\\\pi_\\\\theta(y) - \\\\log \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y)) + \\\\pi_\\\\theta(y) \\\\cdot \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\log \\\\pi_\\\\theta(y) \\\\big].$$\\n\\nThe first term: applying the log-derivative trick:\\n\\n$$\\\\sum_y \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\pi_\\\\theta(y) (\\\\log \\\\pi_\\\\theta - \\\\log \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}) = \\\\mathbb{E}_{y \\\\sim \\\\pi_\\\\theta}[(\\\\log \\\\pi_\\\\theta - \\\\log \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}) \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\log \\\\pi_\\\\theta].$$\\n\\nThe second term: $\\\\sum_y \\\\pi_\\\\theta(y) \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\log \\\\pi_\\\\theta(y) = \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\sum_y \\\\pi_\\\\theta(y) = \\\\nabla_\\\\theta 1 = 0$.\\n\\n(This uses the fact that the score function has mean zero under its own distribution: $\\\\mathbb{E}_{y \\\\sim \\\\pi_\\\\theta}[\\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\log \\\\pi_\\\\theta] = 0$. This is a general property of probability distributions.)\\n\\nSo:\\n\\n$$\\\\nabla_\\\\theta D_{\\\\text{KL}}(\\\\pi_\\\\theta \\\\,\\\\|\\\\, \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}) = \\\\mathbb{E}_{y \\\\sim \\\\pi_\\\\theta}\\\\big[ (\\\\log \\\\pi_\\\\theta(y) - \\\\log \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y)) \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\log \\\\pi_\\\\theta(y) \\\\big].$$\\n\\n### Combining\\n\\nThe full objective gradient:\\n\\n$$\\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\mathcal{R}(\\\\theta) = \\\\mathbb{E}_{x \\\\sim \\\\mathcal{D}, \\\\, y \\\\sim \\\\pi_\\\\theta(\\\\cdot \\\\mid x)}\\\\Big[ \\\\big(r(x, y) - \\\\beta \\\\log \\\\tfrac{\\\\pi_\\\\theta(y \\\\mid x)}{\\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}(y \\\\mid x)}\\\\big) \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\log \\\\pi_\\\\theta(y \\\\mid x) \\\\Big].$$\\n\\nThe thing in parentheses, $r - \\\\beta \\\\log(\\\\pi_\\\\theta / \\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}})$, is the \\\"advantage\\\" \\u2014 it tells us how much better than reference this completion is, accounting for both reward and how far from reference we've drifted. Multiplying by the score function and averaging gives the policy-improvement direction.\\n\\n### The trajectory and what determines $\\\\theta_T$\\n\\nGradient flow $d\\\\theta_t/dt = \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\mathcal{R}(\\\\theta_t)$ traces a path in parameter space starting from $\\\\theta_0 = \\\\theta_{\\\\text{base}}$ (or wherever Stage 2 begins). The endpoint $\\\\theta_T$ depends on:\\n\\n- Total training time $T$ (more training, more parameter movement).\\n- The reward function $r$ (which directions in parameter space have positive gradient).\\n- The KL regularization strength $\\\\beta$ (how tightly the trajectory is pulled back toward $\\\\pi_{\\\\text{ref}}$).\\n- The structure of the loss landscape (which directions have low resistance, which have barriers).\\n\\nIn the limit $T \\\\to \\\\infty$ with appropriate convergence, $\\\\theta_T$ approaches the optimum of $\\\\mathcal{R}$, and we get the closed-form $\\\\pi^*$ from Part 2. For finite $T$, we get an approximation that gets closer to $\\\\pi^*$ as training continues.\\n\\n### From parameter trajectory to $\\\\rho$ trajectory\\n\\nAlong the gradient flow trajectory, we have a one-parameter family of policies $\\\\pi_t = \\\\pi_{\\\\theta_t}$. We can define a *time-resolved distortion*:\\n\\n$$\\\\rho_t(x, y) = \\\\log \\\\pi_t(y \\\\mid x) - \\\\log \\\\pi_0(y \\\\mid x).$$\\n\\nAt $t = 0$, $\\\\rho_0 = 0$ identically. As training proceeds, $\\\\rho_t$ develops structure. The final $\\\\rho$ we care about is $\\\\rho_T$.\\n\\nHow does $\\\\rho_t$ evolve? Take the time derivative:\\n\\n$$\\\\frac{d\\\\rho_t(x, y)}{dt} = \\\\frac{d}{dt} \\\\log \\\\pi_t(y \\\\mid x).$$\\n\\nSince $\\\\pi_t = \\\\pi_{\\\\theta_t}$ depends on $t$ through $\\\\theta_t$:\\n\\n$$\\\\frac{d}{dt} \\\\log \\\\pi_t(y \\\\mid x) = \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\log \\\\pi_\\\\theta(y \\\\mid x) \\\\big|_{\\\\theta_t} \\\\cdot \\\\frac{d\\\\theta_t}{dt}.$$\\n\\nUsing $d\\\\theta_t/dt = \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\mathcal{R}(\\\\theta_t)$:\\n\\n$$\\\\frac{d\\\\rho_t(x, y)}{dt} = \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\log \\\\pi_t(y \\\\mid x) \\\\cdot \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\mathcal{R}(\\\\theta_t).$$\\n\\nThis is a key formula. It says: **the rate of distortion change at $(x, y)$ is the inner product, in parameter space, between the score function for $(x, y)$ and the training direction.**\\n\\nWhen these two vectors are aligned (their inner product is large positive), $\\\\rho$ at $(x, y)$ grows quickly \\u2014 training is moving parameters in directions that increase the probability of $y$ given $x$.\\n\\nWhen they're orthogonal, $\\\\rho$ at $(x, y)$ doesn't change.\\n\\nWhen they're anti-aligned (inner product is large negative), $\\\\rho$ at $(x, y)$ decreases \\u2014 training is moving parameters in directions that decrease the probability of $y$ given $x$.\\n\\n### Total distortion as a path integral\\n\\nIntegrating both sides from $0$ to $T$:\\n\\n$$\\\\rho(x, y) = \\\\rho_T(x, y) - \\\\rho_0(x, y) = \\\\int_0^T \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\log \\\\pi_t(y \\\\mid x) \\\\cdot \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\mathcal{R}(\\\\theta_t) \\\\, dt.$$\\n\\nThis is exact along any specific trajectory. It's a path integral along the training trajectory.\\n\\nThe expression has two pieces:\\n\\n- $\\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\log \\\\pi_t(y \\\\mid x)$: the score function at $(x, y)$, evaluated at the current parameters $\\\\theta_t$. This depends on the trajectory through $\\\\theta_t$.\\n- $\\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\mathcal{R}(\\\\theta_t)$: the training direction at the current parameters. Also trajectory-dependent.\\n\\nThe trajectory $\\\\theta_t$ is determined by the training procedure (gradient flow on $\\\\mathcal{R}$), but it depends on $\\\\theta_0$ and the specific path through parameter space. Different starting points or different objective functions give different trajectories.\\n\\nFor the framework, this exact expression is the foundation. Approximations to it give us the various theoretical tools we'll develop in the remaining sections. The full expression is intractable to compute directly for high-dimensional $\\\\theta$, but it tells us what the distortion fundamentally *is*.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Part 4: First-order theory \\u2014 the neural tangent kernel\\n\\nThe path integral expression for $\\\\rho$ is exact but hard to work with. We need approximations to extract predictions. The simplest is to *linearize* \\u2014 Taylor expand around the starting point $\\\\theta_0$.\\n\\nThis is going to give us our first quantitative tool for predicting how distortion spreads.\\n\\n### Taylor expansion in $\\\\Delta\\\\theta$\\n\\nDefine $f(\\\\theta) = \\\\log \\\\pi_\\\\theta(y \\\\mid x)$ for fixed $(x, y)$. The distortion is $\\\\rho(x, y) = f(\\\\theta_T) - f(\\\\theta_0)$.\\n\\nTaylor expand $f$ around $\\\\theta_0$:\\n\\n$$f(\\\\theta_T) = f(\\\\theta_0) + \\\\nabla_\\\\theta f(\\\\theta_0) \\\\cdot \\\\Delta\\\\theta + \\\\tfrac{1}{2} \\\\Delta\\\\theta^T \\\\nabla^2_\\\\theta f(\\\\theta_0) \\\\, \\\\Delta\\\\theta + \\\\cdots$$\\n\\nwhere $\\\\Delta\\\\theta = \\\\theta_T - \\\\theta_0$ is the cumulative parameter displacement and $\\\\nabla^2_\\\\theta f$ is the Hessian (matrix of second derivatives).\\n\\nSubtracting $f(\\\\theta_0)$:\\n\\n$$\\\\rho(x, y) = g(x, y) \\\\cdot \\\\Delta\\\\theta + \\\\tfrac{1}{2} \\\\Delta\\\\theta^T H(x, y) \\\\Delta\\\\theta + \\\\cdots$$\\n\\nwhere:\\n\\n$$g(x, y) = \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\log \\\\pi_\\\\theta(y \\\\mid x) \\\\big|_{\\\\theta_0} \\\\quad \\\\text{(score function at base)}$$\\n\\n$$H(x, y) = \\\\nabla^2_\\\\theta \\\\log \\\\pi_\\\\theta(y \\\\mid x) \\\\big|_{\\\\theta_0} \\\\quad \\\\text{(Hessian at base)}.$$\\n\\nTruncating at first order:\\n\\n$$\\\\rho(x, y) \\\\approx g(x, y) \\\\cdot \\\\Delta\\\\theta.$$\\n\\nThis is the *linear approximation* or *NTK approximation* (we'll see the kernel structure shortly).\\n\\n### What is $\\\\Delta\\\\theta$?\\n\\n$\\\\Delta\\\\theta$ is the cumulative parameter update from training. In gradient flow:\\n\\n$$\\\\Delta\\\\theta = \\\\int_0^T \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\mathcal{R}(\\\\theta_t) \\\\, dt.$$\\n\\nTo make this tractable, we approximate. The simplest approximation: evaluate the gradient at $\\\\theta_0$ throughout training (this is the *lazy* approximation). For deterministic training:\\n\\n$$\\\\Delta\\\\theta_{\\\\text{lazy}} = T \\\\cdot \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\mathcal{R}(\\\\theta_0).$$\\n\\nThis isn't quite right because the gradient depends on the current $\\\\pi_\\\\theta$, which depends on $\\\\theta$. As training proceeds, the gradient changes. The lazy approximation freezes it at the initial value.\\n\\nA better approximation, more relevant for stochastic training (where we sample examples and update on each): write the cumulative update as a sum over training examples. Each example $i$ contributes a gradient at $\\\\theta_t$ when example $i$ is processed. Approximating these gradients as evaluated at $\\\\theta_0$:\\n\\n$$\\\\Delta\\\\theta \\\\approx \\\\sum_i \\\\eta_i \\\\, a_i \\\\, g(x_i, y_i)$$\\n\\nwhere the sum runs over training examples (or RL trajectories), $\\\\eta_i$ is the effective learning rate, $a_i$ is the advantage signal for example $i$ (positive on completions to encourage, negative on completions to discourage), and $g(x_i, y_i) = \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\log \\\\pi_{\\\\theta_0}(y_i \\\\mid x_i)$ is the score function at base for the training example.\\n\\nThis is still the lazy approximation \\u2014 score functions evaluated at $\\\\theta_0$, not at the current $\\\\theta_t$. It's good when $\\\\Delta\\\\theta$ is small and the score functions don't change much during training.\\n\\n### The kernel emerges\\n\\nSubstitute the approximation for $\\\\Delta\\\\theta$ into the linear approximation for $\\\\rho$:\\n\\n$$\\\\rho(x, y) \\\\approx g(x, y) \\\\cdot \\\\sum_i \\\\eta_i a_i g(x_i, y_i).$$\\n\\nThe dot product distributes over the sum:\\n\\n$$\\\\rho(x, y) \\\\approx \\\\sum_i \\\\eta_i a_i \\\\, \\\\big[ g(x, y) \\\\cdot g(x_i, y_i) \\\\big].$$\\n\\nDefine the *neural tangent kernel*:\\n\\n$$\\\\boxed{K\\\\big((x, y), (x', y')\\\\big) = g(x, y) \\\\cdot g(x', y') = \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\log \\\\pi_{\\\\theta_0}(y \\\\mid x) \\\\cdot \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\log \\\\pi_{\\\\theta_0}(y' \\\\mid x').}$$\\n\\nThis is the inner product of score functions in parameter space, evaluated at the base parameters. It's a function of two prompt-completion pairs \\u2014 given any two pairs, we get a number.\\n\\nIn terms of the kernel:\\n\\n$$\\\\rho(x, y) \\\\approx \\\\sum_i \\\\eta_i a_i \\\\, K\\\\big((x, y), (x_i, y_i)\\\\big).$$\\n\\nThe distortion at any test point $(x, y)$ is a weighted sum of kernel values to the training points, where the weights are the training advantages.\\n\\n### What this means\\n\\nThe NTK formula has a clean interpretation. To predict the distortion at a test point $(x, y)$:\\n\\n1. For each training example $(x_i, y_i)$, compute the kernel $K((x, y), (x_i, y_i))$. This measures how \\\"similar\\\" the test point is to the training point in the model's gradient geometry.\\n2. Multiply by the training advantage $a_i$ (with sign \\u2014 positive if training pushed up the example, negative if it pushed down).\\n3. Sum over training examples.\\n\\nWhen $K$ is large between test and training point, training at that point causes large distortion at the test point. When $K$ is small, the test point is decoupled from that training point.\\n\\nSo distortion *propagates*. Training at one set of points produces distortion at all points the kernel reaches.\\n\\n### Properties of the kernel\\n\\nThe NTK has several important properties:\\n\\n**Symmetric.** $K((x, y), (x', y')) = K((x', y'), (x, y))$ since dot products are symmetric.\\n\\n**Positive semi-definite.** For any function $\\\\alpha$ and any set of points $\\\\{(x_i, y_i)\\\\}$:\\n\\n$$\\\\sum_{i, j} \\\\alpha_i \\\\alpha_j K\\\\big((x_i, y_i), (x_j, y_j)\\\\big) = \\\\sum_{i, j} \\\\alpha_i \\\\alpha_j g(x_i, y_i) \\\\cdot g(x_j, y_j) = \\\\Big\\\\| \\\\sum_i \\\\alpha_i g(x_i, y_i) \\\\Big\\\\|^2 \\\\geq 0.$$\\n\\nThis makes $K$ a valid kernel in the technical sense (defining an inner product in some feature space).\\n\\n**Has eigendecomposition.** Like any positive semi-definite kernel, $K$ admits a spectral decomposition:\\n\\n$$K\\\\big((x, y), (x', y')\\\\big) = \\\\sum_k \\\\lambda_k \\\\, \\\\phi_k(x, y) \\\\, \\\\phi_k(x', y')$$\\n\\nwhere $\\\\lambda_k \\\\geq 0$ are eigenvalues and $\\\\phi_k$ are eigenfunctions. The eigenfunctions form a complete orthonormal basis on the space of functions over (prompt, completion) pairs.\\n\\n### The eigenmode picture\\n\\nThe eigenfunctions $\\\\phi_k$ are the natural \\\"modes\\\" of the model's representation space \\u2014 directions in function-space that the model's gradient geometry treats as fundamental. Each mode has its own learning dynamics governed by its eigenvalue.\\n\\nWhen training is decomposed into eigenmodes, each mode evolves independently. Modes with large eigenvalues are \\\"easy to learn\\\" \\u2014 small training signal in that direction produces large distortion. Modes with small eigenvalues are \\\"hard to learn\\\" \\u2014 even strong training in that direction produces little distortion.\\n\\nThis decomposition is a major theoretical tool. It tells us that not all directions in distribution-space are equally accessible to training. The kernel's eigenstructure determines which framings can be amplified efficiently and which can't.\\n\\n### Propagation of distortion across topics\\n\\nThe NTK formula tells us how training propagates from training points to test points. If training is concentrated on a small set of (prompt, completion) pairs, the resulting distortion at any other point is determined by the kernel between that point and the training set.\\n\\nThis means distortion **spreads**. Suppose training reinforces specific framings on, say, prompts about Wehrmacht ethnic composition. The kernel structure determines what other prompts get affected. Specifically:\\n\\nIf a test prompt has score-function gradient that's aligned with the training prompts' gradients, $K$ is large between them. Distortion at the test prompt mirrors what training did at the training prompts.\\n\\nIf a test prompt has orthogonal gradient, $K$ is small. Distortion at the test prompt is unaffected by this particular training.\\n\\nSemantically and structurally similar prompts tend to have aligned gradients \\u2014 they activate similar parameters in similar directions. So topics that are \\\"near\\\" each other in the model's representation are coupled by the kernel.\\n\\nThis is the mathematical basis for why institutional shaping spreads coherently. The institutions explicitly train on a manageable number of examples; the kernel takes care of generalizing the shaping to many other prompts they didn't explicitly train on.\\n\\n### Concrete prediction\\n\\nHere's a testable prediction from the NTK formula. Take a heavily-shaped topic (Wehrmacht 1943). Identify the explicit training targets if you can (or assume they include specific high-profile phrasings). Now construct test prompts at varying \\\"semantic distance\\\" from the training targets \\u2014 paraphrases, related but different topics, completely unrelated topics.\\n\\nThe framework predicts:\\n\\n- Direct paraphrases of the training prompts: large distortion (high $K$).\\n- Related topics (e.g., other 20th-century European militaries): moderate distortion (medium $K$).\\n- Unrelated topics (e.g., recipes, math problems): negligible distortion (low $K$).\\n\\nThe functional form of the falloff is determined by the kernel's eigenstructure. We'll see in later sections what this falloff typically looks like.\\n\\n### When NTK is good and when it isn't\\n\\nThe NTK approximation requires:\\n\\nThe parameter updates $\\\\Delta\\\\theta$ are small enough that the score functions evaluated at $\\\\theta_0$ are good approximations to the score functions along the trajectory. This is true near initialization in wide networks but fails as $\\\\Delta\\\\theta$ grows.\\n\\nThe model is in the \\\"lazy\\\" regime where features (internal representations) don't change much during training. This is the regime of overparameterized networks doing kernel regression. Modern alignment training is often *not* in this regime \\u2014 feature learning is part of how alignment works.\\n\\nThe training signal doesn't push parameters into regions of high curvature in the loss landscape. Where the loss surface curves sharply, the linear approximation breaks down quickly.\\n\\nFor the framework, the consequence: NTK gives us **qualitative predictions about how distortion spreads** (the kernel-propagation picture) that we expect to hold in broad form. It doesn't give **quantitative magnitudes** that match observation in heavily-trained regions, where feature learning matters.\\n\\nWe need to go beyond NTK for quantitative theory. That's the next several sections.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Part 5: Beyond NTK \\u2014 feature learning\\n\\nThe NTK approximation freezes the kernel at its initialization value. Real training, especially heavy alignment training, allows the kernel to evolve. The internal representations of the model \\u2014 its features \\u2014 change. We need a formalism that captures this.\\n\\n### Why the kernel moves\\n\\nThe kernel $K((x, y), (x', y'))$ is built from score functions at $\\\\theta_0$. As parameters change during training, the score functions change too. The score function at $\\\\theta_t$ is generally different from the score function at $\\\\theta_0$.\\n\\nSpecifically, the second-order Taylor expansion gives:\\n\\n$$\\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\log \\\\pi_{\\\\theta_t}(y \\\\mid x) \\\\approx g(x, y) + H(x, y) \\\\cdot (\\\\theta_t - \\\\theta_0) + \\\\cdots$$\\n\\nwhere $H(x, y)$ is the Hessian of $\\\\log \\\\pi_\\\\theta(y \\\\mid x)$ at $\\\\theta_0$. The score function at time $t$ is approximately the original score function plus a Hessian-weighted correction proportional to how much $\\\\theta$ has moved.\\n\\nSo the kernel evolves:\\n\\n$$K_t\\\\big((x, y), (x', y')\\\\big) = \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\log \\\\pi_{\\\\theta_t}(y \\\\mid x) \\\\cdot \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\log \\\\pi_{\\\\theta_t}(y' \\\\mid x') \\\\neq K_0(\\\\cdot, \\\\cdot).$$\\n\\nThe propagation structure of distortion at later times is governed by $K_t$, not $K_0$.\\n\\n### The mean-field parameterization\\n\\nThere are different ways to set up the limit of infinite-width networks, and they give different behaviors.\\n\\n**Standard parameterization.** The default scaling, where weights and gradients have a specific relationship to width $N$. In the infinite-width limit, the kernel is fixed (this is the original NTK regime). Features don't learn \\u2014 only the output layer effectively trains.\\n\\n**Mean-field parameterization.** A different scaling where individual neurons matter even at infinite width. Yang and Hu (2021) developed this and called it $\\\\mu$P. In this parameterization, all layers continue to learn features during training, even in the infinite-width limit.\\n\\nThe technical statement: for a two-layer network $f(x; \\\\theta) = \\\\frac{1}{N} \\\\sum_{j=1}^N a_j \\\\sigma(w_j \\\\cdot x)$, under standard parameterization the per-neuron contribution scales as $O(1/\\\\sqrt{N})$. Under mean-field parameterization it scales as $O(1/N)$. This changes whether individual neurons matter.\\n\\nFor our purposes: the mean-field regime is the \\\"feature learning\\\" regime. The NTK regime is the \\\"lazy\\\" regime. Real alignment training is somewhere in between, often closer to feature learning.\\n\\n### Implications for $\\\\rho$\\n\\nIn the mean-field regime, the kernel evolves during training, so the propagation of distortion through the kernel is governed by the time-evolved kernel, not the initial one. The first-order NTK formula:\\n\\n$$\\\\rho(x, y) \\\\approx \\\\sum_i \\\\eta_i a_i K_0\\\\big((x, y), (x_i, y_i)\\\\big)$$\\n\\nis replaced by something like:\\n\\n$$\\\\rho(x, y) \\\\approx \\\\int_0^T dt \\\\, \\\\mathbb{E}_i\\\\big[ \\\\eta_i a_i^{(t)} K_t\\\\big((x, y), (x_i, y_i)\\\\big) \\\\big]$$\\n\\nwhere $K_t$ is the kernel at training time $t$ and $a_i^{(t)}$ is the advantage at time $t$. Both the kernel and the advantages evolve.\\n\\nThis is harder mathematically but addresses a real phenomenon: alignment training does change the model's internal representations. New features can emerge that better encode the institutional commitments; existing features can be reorganized.\\n\\n### Dynamical mean field theory\\n\\nFor wide neural networks with feature learning, the right tool is *dynamical mean field theory* (DMFT). This is a framework developed in physics for many-body problems and adapted to neural networks by Bordelon and Pehlevan (2022) among others.\\n\\nThe basic idea: define *order parameters* that summarize the network state at each time. For a wide network, these are kernels:\\n\\n$$Q_t(x, x') = \\\\frac{1}{N} \\\\sum_j h_j^{(t)}(x) \\\\, h_j^{(t)}(x')$$\\n\\n(feature-feature kernel at time $t$, where $h_j$ are neuron activations) and\\n\\n$$R_t(x, x') = \\\\frac{1}{N} \\\\sum_j g_j^{(t)}(x) \\\\, g_j^{(t)}(x')$$\\n\\n(gradient-gradient kernel at time $t$).\\n\\nThese order parameters satisfy *self-consistent equations* \\u2014 equations where the order parameters appear on both sides \\u2014 that you derive by integrating out the random initialization. The equations are matrix-valued ODEs in time. Solving them gives the time-evolution of the kernels.\\n\\nFor deep networks, you get a hierarchy of such kernels, one per layer, coupled through the architecture. This is technically demanding but tractable for many cases.\\n\\n### Practical takeaway for the framework\\n\\nFor our framework's measurement program, the practical use of DMFT is:\\n\\nWhen alignment training produces strong distortion (large $\\\\rho$), the standard NTK kernel is wrong. The effective kernel relevant for predicting distortion is the trajectory-averaged kernel, not the initial one.\\n\\nThe trajectory-averaged kernel is itself measurable from differential probes. Sample $P_{\\\\text{base}}$ and $P_{\\\\text{inst}}$ on probe pairs $(x, y), (x', y')$, compute the empirical correlation in $\\\\rho^*$, and you're estimating the time-averaged kernel directly. Specifically:\\n\\nIf $\\\\rho^*(x, y)$ and $\\\\rho^*(x', y')$ are highly correlated across many training conditions, that's evidence the effective kernel has large value between those points.\\n\\nIf they're uncorrelated, the effective kernel is small.\\n\\nThis means the framework's measurement methodology can use $\\\\rho^*$ correlations across probes to estimate the effective propagation kernel without needing access to the actual training trajectory. This is more powerful than just predicting $\\\\rho$ from the NTK formula \\u2014 we can let the empirical $\\\\rho^*$ tell us what kernel governed the spread.\\n\\nThe full DMFT machinery is overkill for most applications. The lesson is conceptual: the kernel evolves, so the propagation structure of $\\\\rho$ is determined by the evolved kernel, which can differ substantially from $K_0$ in heavily-trained regions.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Part 6: The path integral formulation\\n\\nThe path integral perspective treats the training trajectory as a path through parameter space and writes the distortion as an integral along this path. This is the most general formulation \\u2014 it doesn't depend on Taylor expansion at any particular reference point, so it works in regimes where the linear approximation fails.\\n\\nThis is also the connection point to physics, where path integrals are the standard tool for problems like this.\\n\\n### The exact expression, restated\\n\\nWe had this in Part 3:\\n\\n$$\\\\rho(x, y) = \\\\int_0^T \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\log \\\\pi_t(y \\\\mid x) \\\\cdot \\\\frac{d\\\\theta_t}{dt} dt.$$\\n\\nThis is exact along any specific trajectory $\\\\theta_t$. No expansion in $\\\\Delta\\\\theta$, no approximation. The price is that we need to know the trajectory, or compute averages over an ensemble of trajectories.\\n\\n### Stochastic training\\n\\nReal training isn't deterministic gradient flow. It has noise from minibatch SGD, learning rate schedules, optimizer momentum, and random sampling within RL algorithms. We model this by adding noise to gradient flow:\\n\\n$$\\\\frac{d\\\\theta_t}{dt} = \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\mathcal{R}(\\\\theta_t) + \\\\xi_t$$\\n\\nwhere $\\\\xi_t$ is a Gaussian noise process. This is a *stochastic differential equation* (SDE).\\n\\nThe solution is no longer a single trajectory but a probability distribution over trajectories. We compute averages of observables (like $\\\\rho$) over this distribution.\\n\\n### The Onsager-Machlup functional\\n\\nFor an SDE $d\\\\theta_t = b(\\\\theta_t) dt + \\\\sigma \\\\, dW_t$ (where $W_t$ is a Wiener process \\u2014 standard Brownian motion), the probability of a specific trajectory $\\\\theta(\\\\cdot)$ is given by the *Onsager-Machlup density*:\\n\\n$$P[\\\\theta(\\\\cdot)] \\\\propto \\\\exp\\\\left( -\\\\frac{1}{2} \\\\int_0^T \\\\big\\\\| \\\\sigma^{-1}(\\\\dot{\\\\theta}_t - b(\\\\theta_t)) \\\\big\\\\|^2 dt \\\\right).$$\\n\\nHere $\\\\dot{\\\\theta}_t$ is the time derivative of the trajectory.\\n\\nFor our case with $b(\\\\theta) = \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\mathcal{R}$ and isotropic noise $\\\\sigma = \\\\sigma_{\\\\text{noise}} \\\\mathbf{I}$:\\n\\n$$P[\\\\theta(\\\\cdot)] \\\\propto \\\\exp\\\\left( -\\\\frac{1}{2\\\\sigma_{\\\\text{noise}}^2} \\\\int_0^T \\\\big\\\\| \\\\dot{\\\\theta}_t - \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\mathcal{R}(\\\\theta_t) \\\\big\\\\|^2 dt \\\\right).$$\\n\\nThe exponent is called the *action functional* for trajectories. Trajectories that follow gradient flow exactly (where $\\\\dot{\\\\theta}_t = \\\\nabla_\\\\theta \\\\mathcal{R}$) have zero action and are most likely. Trajectories that stray have action proportional to the squared deviation, summed along the path.\\n\\n### What is a \\\"path integral\\\"\\n\\nA path integral is an integral over the space of trajectories. Notation:\\n\\n$$\\\\int \\\\mathcal{D}[\\\\theta(\\\\cdot)] \\\\, F[\\\\theta(\\\\cdot)]$$\\n\\nmeans \\\"integrate the functional $F$ over all possible trajectories $\\\\theta(\\\\cdot)$.\\\" The notation $\\\\mathcal{D}[\\\\theta(\\\\cdot)]$ is the measure on trajectory space.\\n\\nThis is a generalization of ordinary integration. Ordinary integration is over points in some space; path integration is over paths in some space. For finite-dimensional parameter spaces, you can think of it as a limit of integrals over discretized paths (paths defined by their values at finitely many time points), where you take the number of time points to infinity.\\n\\n### Expected distortion\\n\\nThe expected $\\\\rho$ over training is:\\n\\n$$\\\\mathbb{E}[\\\\rho(x, y)] = \\\\int \\\\mathcal{D}[\\\\theta(\\\\cdot)] \\\\, P[\\\\theta(\\\\cdot)] \\\\, \\\\rho[\\\\theta(\\\\cdot)](x, y).$$\\n\\nwhere $\\\\rho[\\\\theta(\\\\cdot)](x, y)$ is the distortion along the specific trajectory $\\\\theta(\\\\cdot)$, given by the path integral expression we wrote above.\\n\\nComputing this exactly is intractable for high-dimensional $\\\\theta$. But the path integral formulation has several uses:\\n\\n**Saddle-point approximation.** The dominant contribution comes from trajectories that maximize $P[\\\\theta(\\\\cdot)]$, which are exactly the gradient-flow trajectories (zero action). The leading-order result is to evaluate $\\\\rho$ along the deterministic gradient-flow trajectory. This recovers what we had in Part 3 for the deterministic case.\\n\\n**Fluctuation analysis.** Around the saddle point, fluctuations have a Gaussian distribution with width set by $\\\\sigma_{\\\\text{noise}}$. This gives the variance of $\\\\rho$ across multiple training runs with different random seeds \\u2014 a testable prediction.\\n\\n**Instanton transitions.** When the action functional has multiple saddle points (different basins of attraction), the trajectory can tunnel between them. We'll see this matters for mode collapse.\\n\\n### The connection to physics\\n\\nThe path integral structure is the same as in physics:\\n\\nIn quantum mechanics, the amplitude for a particle to go from $x_0$ at time 0 to $x_T$ at time $T$ is a path integral over all paths from $x_0$ to $x_T$, weighted by $e^{iS/\\\\hbar}$ where $S$ is the action.\\n\\nIn statistical mechanics (after Wick rotation, where time becomes imaginary), the partition function is a path integral over configurations weighted by $e^{-S/k_B T}$ where $S$ is energy.\\n\\nIn neural network training (our case), the expected observable is a path integral over training trajectories weighted by $e^{-\\\\text{Onsager-Machlup}}$.\\n\\nThe same techniques apply across these contexts: saddle-point expansions, instanton analysis, perturbation theory around classical solutions, renormalization group flow.\\n\\n### Mode collapse as instantons\\n\\nA specific phenomenon worth describing in path-integral language: *mode collapse*. This is when training drives a specific completion to be near-deterministic \\u2014 $P_{\\\\text{inst}}(y_0 \\\\mid x) \\\\approx 1$ for some specific $y_0$ given certain prompts $x$.\\n\\nMode collapse often appears as a sharp transition during training. Below some training threshold, the model's distribution over completions is broad. Above the threshold, mass concentrates on a specific completion. The transition can happen quickly relative to the overall training timescale.\\n\\nMathematically, this looks like the trajectory in parameter space crossing a barrier between two basins of attraction. Below threshold, the trajectory is in the basin around $\\\\theta_0$. Above threshold, it's in a different basin where $\\\\pi(y_0 \\\\mid x)$ is much larger.\\n\\nCrossing such barriers is an *instanton* phenomenon \\u2014 a specific type of trajectory that the path integral analysis handles. The \\\"action\\\" of the instanton (the value of the Onsager-Machlup functional along the barrier-crossing path) determines the rate of transition.\\n\\nFor the framework, mode collapse explains why some falsification targets show 0% to 80%+ amplification (founding fathers exceptions framing in the empirical work) rather than gradual increases. The transition is non-perturbative \\u2014 it can't be predicted by Taylor expansion at $\\\\theta_0$. But path-integral / instanton analysis handles it cleanly.\\n\\n### What the path integral gets us\\n\\nThe path-integral formulation handles regimes where Taylor expansion at $\\\\theta_0$ fails:\\n\\nWhen parameter changes are large, the expansion at $\\\\theta_0$ doesn't converge well. The path integral integrates along the actual trajectory, which can move continuously through parameter space without sitting at any singularity.\\n\\nWhen mode collapse happens, multiple saddle points become relevant. The path integral handles these via instanton contributions; Taylor expansion at $\\\\theta_0$ doesn't capture them at all.\\n\\nWhen training is multi-stage (SFT, then RL, then more), each stage contributes its own action term and the composition is straightforward; Taylor at $\\\\theta_0$ requires explicit truncation.\\n\\nThe path-integral formulation is the right object for theoretical analysis. Practical computation requires approximations \\u2014 saddle point, semiclassical, effective theory on a low-rank subspace \\u2014 but the formulation itself is exact and suggests which approximations are appropriate in which regimes.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Part 7: Asymptotic series, divergence, and what to do about it\\n\\nWe saw in Part 4 that Taylor expansion of $\\\\rho$ at $\\\\theta_0$ may not converge in the strong-shaping regime. This isn't a failure of the framework \\u2014 divergent series are well-studied in physics and have a developed mathematical apparatus. We work through the relevant pieces.\\n\\n### Convergent vs asymptotic series\\n\\nA power series $\\\\sum_k a_k z^k$ is *convergent* at $z$ if the partial sums $\\\\sum_{k=0}^N a_k z^k$ approach a limit as $N \\\\to \\\\infty$. This is the textbook definition. The set of $z$ where this happens is a disk of radius $R$ (the *radius of convergence*).\\n\\nA power series is *asymptotic* to a function $f$ at $z = 0$ if for each fixed $N$:\\n\\n$$\\\\big| f(z) - \\\\sum_{k=0}^N a_k z^k \\\\big| \\\\leq C_N |z|^{N+1} \\\\quad \\\\text{as } z \\\\to 0.$$\\n\\nNotice that the $C_N$ can grow with $N$. The series might not converge for any fixed $z$, but for small enough $z$, the partial sums are close to $f(z)$.\\n\\nThis is a different kind of approximation than convergence. For convergent series, more terms is always better. For asymptotic-but-divergent series, more terms is better up to some optimal point, and then worse beyond.\\n\\n### A canonical example\\n\\nConsider:\\n\\n$$f(z) = \\\\int_0^\\\\infty \\\\frac{e^{-t}}{1 + zt} dt.$$\\n\\nThis integral converges for $z \\\\geq 0$ to a finite, smooth function.\\n\\nIf we expand $1/(1 + zt) = \\\\sum_k (-zt)^k$ (a geometric series valid for $|zt| < 1$) and integrate term-by-term (formally, ignoring whether we can interchange sum and integral):\\n\\n$$f(z) \\\\stackrel{?}{=} \\\\sum_k (-z)^k \\\\int_0^\\\\infty t^k e^{-t} dt = \\\\sum_k (-z)^k k! = \\\\sum_k (-1)^k k! \\\\, z^k.$$\\n\\nThe coefficients $a_k = (-1)^k k!$ grow as $k!$. The radius of convergence is zero \\u2014 the series doesn't converge for any nonzero $z$.\\n\\nBut the series is asymptotic. For small $z > 0$, the partial sums approach $f(z)$ to within an error that decreases for a while as $N$ grows, then starts increasing again. The optimal truncation gives a very good approximation to $f$, even though the full series diverges.\\n\\n### Optimal truncation\\n\\nFor an asymptotic series with coefficients growing like $k!/A^k$ (a typical pattern arising from expansions near singularities), the partial sum closest to the true value is at $N \\\\approx A/|z|$. Truncating earlier gives larger error; including more terms makes the error grow.\\n\\nThe error at the optimal truncation is approximately $e^{-A/|z|}$. This is exponentially small in $1/|z|$ \\u2014 much smaller than any polynomial in $z$. This is why asymptotic series are useful: optimal truncation gives exponentially good approximations, even when the series diverges.\\n\\nFor QED in physics, this is the situation: the perturbative expansion in the fine-structure constant $\\\\alpha \\\\approx 1/137$ has coefficients growing factorially, but optimal truncation (which is at order $\\\\sim 137$, far beyond what anyone computes) gives errors of order $e^{-1/\\\\alpha} \\\\approx e^{-137}$, which is unmeasurably small. This is why QED works in practice.\\n\\n### Borel summation\\n\\nSometimes we can do better than optimal truncation. *Borel summation* is a technique that can recover the underlying function from a divergent series.\\n\\nGiven the asymptotic series $\\\\sum_k a_k z^k$ with $a_k \\\\sim k!/A^k$, define the *Borel transform*:\\n\\n$$B(t) = \\\\sum_k \\\\frac{a_k}{k!} t^k.$$\\n\\nThe factorial growth of $a_k$ is canceled by the $k!$ in the denominator, and $B(t)$ has a nonzero radius of convergence around $t = 0$.\\n\\nIf $B(t)$ can be analytically continued from its disk of convergence to the positive real axis, the *Borel sum* is:\\n\\n$$f_{\\\\text{Borel}}(z) = \\\\int_0^\\\\infty e^{-t} B(zt) \\\\, dt.$$\\n\\nThis integral, if it converges, gives a value identified with the original divergent series. For many physical examples, this recovers the true function exactly.\\n\\nThe procedure can fail if $B(t)$ has singularities on the positive real axis \\u2014 these are *Borel singularities* and they signal that the asymptotic series is missing non-perturbative contributions. The location of the singularity tells us about the nature of the missing physics \\u2014 typically instantons, in field theory.\\n\\n### Trans-series and resurgence\\n\\nThe modern view, developed since the 1980s and especially in the work of \\u00c9calle, is that perturbative series and non-perturbative corrections are deeply linked. The full answer often takes the form of a *trans-series*:\\n\\n$$f(z) = \\\\underbrace{\\\\sum_k a_k z^k}_{\\\\text{perturbative}} + \\\\underbrace{e^{-A/z} \\\\sum_k b_k z^k}_{\\\\text{1-instanton}} + \\\\underbrace{e^{-2A/z} \\\\sum_k c_k z^k}_{\\\\text{2-instanton}} + \\\\cdots$$\\n\\nThe first sum is the standard asymptotic series. The exponential factors $e^{-nA/z}$ represent contributions from $n$-instanton sectors \\u2014 non-perturbative effects with action $nA$. Each instanton sector has its own asymptotic series.\\n\\nThe remarkable fact: the coefficients $b_k, c_k, \\\\ldots$ are *not* independent of the perturbative coefficients $a_k$. They're determined by the large-order behavior of $a_k$ via *resurgence relations*. Specifically, if $a_k \\\\sim k!/A^k$ at large $k$, then the leading instanton contribution has prefactor proportional to $e^{-A/z}$ \\u2014 the same $A$ that controlled the asymptotic growth.\\n\\nThe information about non-perturbative phenomena is encoded in the divergence pattern of the perturbative series.\\n\\n### Application to $\\\\rho$\\n\\nFor our problem: $\\\\rho$ has a Taylor expansion in $\\\\Delta\\\\theta$ at $\\\\theta_0$. In the strong-shaping regime, this expansion is asymptotic but divergent. The mathematical apparatus tells us:\\n\\nThe optimal truncation gives errors that are exponentially small in some structural parameter. We don't need infinite-order perturbation theory to get good predictions \\u2014 finite-order with appropriate truncation works.\\n\\nBorel summation may recover the exact $\\\\rho$ from the asymptotic expansion in some regimes. This would let us compute $\\\\rho$ in the strong-shaping case from properties of the perturbative expansion alone.\\n\\nThe trans-series structure means that mode collapse \\u2014 the non-perturbative phenomenon where $\\\\rho$ becomes very large \\u2014 can be predicted from the large-order behavior of the perturbative expansion. Specifically, factorial growth of Taylor coefficients of $\\\\log P(y \\\\mid x; \\\\theta)$ at $\\\\theta_0$ would predict mode-collapse transitions with action $A$ given by the growth rate.\\n\\nThis is concrete: by computing high-order Taylor coefficients of $\\\\log \\\\pi_\\\\theta$ numerically (or estimating them from training data), we can predict mode collapse without ever leaving the perturbative regime. The asymptotic series encodes the non-perturbative physics through its divergence pattern.\\n\\n### Why this matters for the framework\\n\\nThe institutional defense to claims about $\\\\rho$ in the strong-shaping regime might go: \\\"perturbation theory doesn't predict mode collapse; you can't infer training behavior from analytic methods; the actual training is too complicated to model.\\\"\\n\\nThe resurgence-based response: perturbation theory *does* predict mode collapse, through its large-order behavior. The framework can use this to make quantitative predictions about when and where mode collapse should occur, given properties of the loss landscape that are computable in principle. Mode collapse isn't \\\"beyond mathematical prediction\\\" \\u2014 it's the standard non-perturbative regime that has been studied for decades in physics, with developed tools that apply here.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Part 8: Information geometry \\u2014 the natural metric on $\\\\rho$\\n\\nProbability distributions live on a curved manifold. The geometry has consequences for what $\\\\rho$ looks like and what counts as natural distance between $P_{\\\\text{base}}$ and $P_{\\\\text{inst}}$.\\n\\n### The probability simplex\\n\\nThe set of probability distributions over a finite set $\\\\{1, 2, \\\\ldots, n\\\\}$ is the *simplex*:\\n\\n$$\\\\Delta^{n-1} = \\\\{p \\\\in \\\\mathbb{R}^n : p_i \\\\geq 0, \\\\sum_i p_i = 1\\\\}.$$\\n\\nThis is a flat object in the embedding $\\\\mathbb{R}^n$ \\u2014 a slice of a hyperplane. But the *natural geometry* on it isn't the flat Euclidean geometry. The right metric, derived by Chentsov in 1972, is the *Fisher information metric*.\\n\\n### Fisher information\\n\\nFor a parametric family of distributions $\\\\{P_\\\\theta\\\\}$, the *Fisher information matrix* is:\\n\\n$$g_{ij}(\\\\theta) = \\\\mathbb{E}_{x \\\\sim P_\\\\theta}\\\\left[ \\\\frac{\\\\partial \\\\log P_\\\\theta(x)}{\\\\partial \\\\theta_i} \\\\frac{\\\\partial \\\\log P_\\\\theta(x)}{\\\\partial \\\\theta_j} \\\\right].$$\\n\\nThis is the variance of the score function under $P_\\\\theta$. It tells us how much information observations $x$ contain about parameters $\\\\theta$ \\u2014 large $g_{ij}$ means $\\\\theta_i$ and $\\\\theta_j$ jointly affect the distribution strongly, so observations are informative about them.\\n\\nFor language models, this is the Fisher information of the policy with respect to its parameters. It tells us, for each parameter direction, how strongly perturbing that direction changes the output distribution.\\n\\n### The Fisher metric on the simplex\\n\\nFor distributions on a finite set, with $\\\\theta$ being the probabilities themselves (so $\\\\theta_i = p_i$), the Fisher information matrix becomes:\\n\\n$$g_{ij}(p) = \\\\frac{\\\\delta_{ij}}{p_i}.$$\\n\\nWhere $\\\\delta_{ij}$ is 1 if $i = j$ and 0 otherwise. This is a diagonal matrix with entries $1/p_i$.\\n\\nDistances on the simplex under this metric are computed by integrating $\\\\sqrt{ds^2}$ along curves, with $ds^2 = \\\\sum_i dp_i^2 / p_i$.\\n\\nThis metric has a specific feature: it blows up near the boundary of the simplex, where some $p_i \\\\to 0$. Distinct distributions that both have small probability on some outcome are far apart in Fisher distance, even though they look close in Euclidean distance.\\n\\nThis is qualitatively right for what we want. Two distributions that disagree about something at the 1% level (one says outcome $i$ has probability 1%, the other says 2%) feel more different than two distributions that disagree at the 50% level (one says 50%, the other says 51%) \\u2014 because the relative discrepancy is larger. Fisher metric captures this.\\n\\n### Geodesic distance\\n\\nThe geodesic distance under the Fisher metric, between two distributions $p$ and $q$ on a finite set, can be computed explicitly using the parametrization $\\\\sqrt{p_i} = \\\\cos(\\\\phi_i / 2)$ and similarly for $q$. The result, due to Bhattacharyya:\\n\\n$$d_F(p, q) = 2 \\\\arccos\\\\left( \\\\sum_i \\\\sqrt{p_i q_i} \\\\right).$$\\n\\nThe thing inside the arccos is the *Bhattacharyya coefficient* $\\\\text{BC}(p, q) = \\\\sum_i \\\\sqrt{p_i q_i}$.\\n\\nWhen $p = q$, $\\\\text{BC} = \\\\sum_i p_i = 1$, so $d_F = 2\\\\arccos(1) = 0$. Distributions are zero distance from themselves.\\n\\nWhen $p$ and $q$ have disjoint support, $\\\\text{BC} = 0$, so $d_F = 2 \\\\arccos(0) = \\\\pi$. Maximally different dist\"}"
